Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Tech could narrow the gap between non-invasive brain computer interfaces and implanted solutions like Neuralink.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Researchers based at Ghent University in Belgium and Linköping University in Sweden have published a paper dubbed Electronic Hair: Transforming hair into a scalable bioelectronic interface. It is well known that skin and hair can get in the way of brain signal monitoring equipment, so sometimes hair is shaved and skin abrasively prepared for EEG readings. Furthermore, brain-computer interfaces are sometimes surgically embedded in a human's head for the best possible signal quality. But by treating hair with electronic conductive polymer dyes, scientists think we can create a better, stable, and natural interfacing platform. The idea has been dubbed eHAIR, for short.

eHAIR is claimed to transform hair "from a barrier into a functional bioelectronic interface." One approach that was tested uses highly conductive, low-impedance coatings that are readily applied and removed. Another bottom-up strategy grows conductive polymers directly on single hair strands. These complementary approaches yield stable, low-impedance, and highly conductive interfaces "that enable gel-free, comfortable EEG acquisition and direct compatibility with conventional hardware and human hair and skin," assert the researchers.

The nature of eHAIR creates potential applications beyond passive electrodes. Researchers highlighted that organic electrochemical transistors (OECTs) can be formed using individual hairs or hair bundles. So, the hair(s) can also act as transistor channels and could be used to amplify signals, for example.

Testing has shown that eHAIR facilitates improved signal amplitude, spectral power, and SNR compared to conventional gel-based electrodes. Medical professionals just have to reapply the coating or repeat the polymerization process if hair grows and degrades the electrical contact. This task isn't very complicated, with the scientists likening it to routine household hair coloring techniques.

At the end of their paper, the scientists ponder new opportunities opened by eHAIR technology, such as "scalable, ultracompliant, and user-friendly interfaces for electrophysiology, brain-computer interfaces, and wearable health monitoring."

There are some non-invasive Neuralink competitors in development that appear to be struggling or limited by human hair, skin, and skull barriers between sensors and the brain. Perhaps eHAIR can give these solutions a boost. However, remember that Neuralink implants place electrodes under the skull, actually within brain tissue. The gap may be narrowed, but it is likely still significant.

#10221. How to Add AI Facial Expression Editing to Your Video Pipeline (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Product teams keep asking engineers for the same feature: “Can users fix the reaction in this clip without reshooting it?” It lands on the backlog as “add expression editing,” and it hides a lot of decisions.

This guide compares the realistic integration options in 2026, sketches a pipeline architecture, models costs, includes a small runnable job helper, and covers the consent and audit logging you need before changing a real person’s face. Vendor details change quickly, so check each vendor’s documentation before you build against it.

Information about features, pricing, APIs and policies in this article was checked on November 10, 2026 and may change. Check each vendor’s documentation before you build against it.

Performance transfer is great for animation, but someone has to record a new take for every change. If your promise is “fix the reaction in the footage you already have,” direct editing fits better.

DRAMA is an AI facial changer from SyncMonster. In the web app, users upload a video or image (MP4, MOV, JPG, PNG), pick a face, choose an emotion, and set intensity on a slider from subtle to strong. The app offers four core emotions (Happy, Sad, Angry, Excited) plus presets such as Surprised, Serious, Evil and Intimidating, with the extra presets on paid plans. It supports emotion blending and editing one face in a group shot without touching the others. SyncMonster states that edits keep the original resolution, lighting and details such as glasses and beards, which is a vendor claim you should test on your own footage.

For developers, SyncMonster publishes public API documentation for DRAMA. Here is what those docs state, as checked on November 10, 2026:

A few things to note honestly. The same generate endpoint also serves other SyncMonster features behind their own flags, so read the parameter table carefully. In the version we checked, the parameter table marks an audio input as required on generate, and the drama-only example includes one, so plan for that in your request builder. The emotion values exposed in the API are the four core emotions, and the extra app presets did not appear in the API docs. The docs describe polling only, with no webhooks mentioned, and they do not publish rate limits, an SDK, or a numeric maximum video duration (an error code exists for exceeding a duration limit).

Runway exposes Act-Two through its developer API as the character_performance endpoint. You send a character (image or video) and a driving performance video, and Act-Two transfers facial expression, and optionally body and hand motion, onto the character. Output is 24fps.

Runway’s API pricing guide lists Act-Two at 5 credits per second of output, with credits priced at $0.01 each, so $0.05 per second. Earlier documentation mentioned a 15-credit minimum per generation, and the current pricing page does not list one, so check for minimums before you budget short clips. The documentation also exposes an expression intensity setting to control how strongly the performance is applied.

The catch is the input requirement: every edit needs someone to act the new reaction. That suits character animation, but it is a heavy ask for a user who wants a slightly warmer smile in an interview clip. It also drives one character per generation.

LivePortrait is an open-source portrait animation framework from Kuaishou’s KwaiVGI group. It animates a source image or video using a driving video or a saved motion template, supports video-to-video portrait editing, and offers retargeting controls for eyes and lips.

The README sets up a Python 3.10 conda environment and expects Git and FFmpeg, with an NVIDIA GPU recommended. It also notes that Apple Silicon runs roughly 20x slower than an RTX 4090, and that Humans mode needs PYTORCH_ENABLE_MPS_FALLBACK=1 on a Mac. That matters if you were hoping to prototype on a MacBook and ship the same setup. A basic run adapted from the README looks like this:

Here -s is the source portrait (an image or video) and -d is the driving clip. Retargeting is exposed through additional options and the Gradio interface, so check the README for current names because they change between releases. Also read the repository’s license file before you plan any commercial use.

The Hugging Face Spaces demo is handy for experiments, but production means owning GPU provisioning, queueing, model updates and quality review. You still steer with a driving input rather than a “happier by 30%” control.

Rule of thumb: if expression editing is a feature rather than your product, buy. If you need on-prem processing or model-level changes, build on LivePortrait and budget for ops.

Whatever the backend, the pipeline has five stages.

Here is the shape of the worker loop. This is illustrative pseudocode only, not any vendor’s SDK or real endpoints:

The idempotency and backoff tips below are easy to get wrong, so here is a small vendor-neutral module. The key function hashes the input file together with the parameters, so a retry with the same input maps to the same job. The poller backs off exponentially up to a ceiling and gives up after a maximum wait. It uses only the Python standard library.

A short test confirms the behavior: the same input gives the same key regardless of parameter order, different parameters give a different key, and the poller returns once the state becomes done.

Credits are not a shared currency across vendors, so compare units first and money second.

For prototyping, the DRAMA web app has a free plan (60 credits, 1080p, up to 2 minutes of processing, 2 projects, watermarked output), which is enough to judge quality on your own sample footage before writing integration code. The docs we checked list API billing in credits but do not state a dollar price per API credit, so confirm that with SyncMonster before you finalize a pricing model. Do not assume web-app plan credits convert at the same rate as API credits.

Plan details are as listed by the vendor on November 10, 2026 and can change.

Changing a real person’s expression can change what viewers believe they felt, so treat this as a product requirement.

For a product feature that edits reactions in existing footage, the DRAMA API is the most direct hosted option among those compared here: face detection with thumbnails for selection, per-face edits, and emotion values you set in code, with no driving performance. Go in knowing its current limits: four emotion parameters in the API, polling instead of webhooks, and no published rate limits or SDK. Starting with the free plan on your own clips is a low-risk way to judge quality. Product details and API access for this AI expression changer are on SyncMonster’s site.

Choose the Runway API if your users want to animate characters from an acted performance. Choose LivePortrait if you need on-prem processing or model-level control and have the GPU and ops capacity. Leave Flawless DeepEditor to studio editorial teams, and Photoshop to still-image workflows.

Yes. DRAMA by SyncMonster has a hosted REST API for editing expressions in existing video using emotion values, and Runway offers Act-Two through its API for performance transfer. LivePortrait is open source and can be wrapped in your own service.

Yes, with a direct editing tool. The DRAMA API takes emotion values from 0 to 1 for happy, sad, excited and angry on a selected face. Runway Act-Two and LivePortrait both expect a driving performance or motion template.

Run face detection first, let the user pick a face, then send that face’s ID with the edit request. The DRAMA API returns a face ID and a thumbnail for each detected face for this purpose.

Per vendor docs as of November 10, 2026: the DRAMA API bills 0.5 credit per second for face detection and 1 credit per second for drama generation, while Runway’s Act-Two API bills 5 credits per second at $0.01 per credit. LivePortrait costs whatever your GPU time costs.

Yes, but its README notes Apple Silicon can be about 20x slower than an RTX 4090. An NVIDIA GPU is recommended for anything beyond experiments.

Not a public one. DeepEditor is an Avid Media Composer extension with access by application, aimed at film and TV editorial teams.

Log the requester, consent record, selected face, emotion and intensity, backend used, input and output hashes, credits used, and a timestamp, and keep the original file.

#10212. Your Pose Model Gives You Coordinates, Not Meaning

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Pose detection in the browser is a solved problem. You can load a model, point it at a webcam, and get a skeleton tracking a human body in real time, on a laptop, with no server involved.

And then you have a skeleton that follows someone around the screen, which is a toy.

The gap between that toy and a feature someone would pay for is smaller than most developers expect, and it isn't made of machine learning. It's made of trigonometry you already know. This article builds a working bicep-curl rep counter to show exactly where that line falls — and more importantly, which side of it your bugs live on.

I'm using MediaPipe Pose Landmarker from Google AI Edge, which is Apache-2.0 licensed and runs entirely in the tab. Here's the whole setup:

Point that at a

Thirty-three points. Each one is a normalised coordinate — x and y run 0 to 1 across the frame, z is a relative depth estimate — plus a visibility score saying how confident the model is that it can actually see that joint.

That's the entire output. Coordinates and confidences.

The model has no concept of exercise. It doesn't know what an arm is for. It has never heard of a bicep curl. It reports where it thinks your joints are, thirty-odd times a second, and stops.

Everything that turns that into a product is yours, and it's ordinary code.

A bicep curl is a change in the angle at the elbow. The elbow angle is defined by three of those 33 points: shoulder, elbow, wrist. In MediaPipe's pose topology those are indices 11, 13 and 15 on the left side, 12, 14 and 16 on the right.

Getting the angle is two vectors and a dot product:

This is the same maths you'd use to point a game character in a direction. Nothing about it is machine learning, and that matters for a reason I'll come back to.

One thing to notice: I'm ignoring z entirely. The depth estimate is relative, not metric — it's good enough for working out which parts of a body are nearer the camera, and not good enough to measure anything. For a 2D elbow angle, using it adds noise and buys nothing.

Here's where almost everyone's first attempt breaks.

The naive version looks at the angle and counts whenever the arm is up:

Run that at 30 frames a second and one slow curl gives you about twenty reps. You haven't counted repetitions. You've counted frames in which the arm happened to be bent.

A rep isn't a state. It's a transition between states:

Fifteen lines, and that's the feature. The counter increments exactly once per cycle, on the edge, because the guard on phase makes every other frame in that cycle a no-op.

If you used a single threshold — say, 100 degrees — you'd get a counter that fires repeatedly whenever someone holds their arm near 100 degrees and the estimate jitters by a degree either side. Every tiny fluctuation across the line is a transition, and every transition is a rep.

Two widely separated thresholds give you hysteresis. Once the arm is "up," it has to travel all the way past 150 degrees before the counter will consider another rep. Noise of a few degrees can't produce a count because no amount of jitter near 55 crosses 150.

This is the same trick a thermostat uses to avoid switching the heating on and off every few seconds, and it's the single most useful pattern in this whole category of work. Any time you turn a continuous signal into discrete events, you want two thresholds, not one.

visibility is the field people skip, and skipping it is how you get phantom reps.

When a joint goes out of frame or gets occluded, the model still returns a coordinate for it. It is obliged to return 33 points. What it does is return a guess, with a low visibility score attached — and a guessed wrist position can swing wildly between frames, which looks exactly like a very fast curl.

Returning null and having update bail out means an occluded frame holds the current state rather than corrupting it. The arm stays "up" until the model can actually see it come down.

If you want to be stricter, smooth the angle over the last three or four frames with a rolling median before it reaches the state machine. A median rejects single-frame outliers in a way a mean doesn't.

Once you've written one of these, you start recognising the shape everywhere:

None of those require a different model. They're the same 33 coordinates with different geometry sitting on top, and the geometry is a few dozen lines each.

Here's why I keep insisting on the distinction between coordinates and meaning.

When my rep counter miscounts, I don't retrain anything. I don't collect a dataset, I don't label examples, I don't spin up a training job. I look at two things: my thresholds, and my state machine. One of them is wrong.

That's a normal bug in normal code, and you already know how to find it. You can log the angle. You can set a breakpoint on the transition. You can write a unit test that feeds a synthetic sequence of angles through update and asserts the count — no camera, no model, no fixtures:

The hard part of this problem — turning pixels into joint positions — was solved by a research team and shipped to you as a file you can download. The part that's left is the part you're already good at.

You are not blocked on machine learning knowledge. You were blocked on nobody telling you this was available.

I set delegate: 'GPU' above without comment, and it deserves one. MediaPipe Tasks lets you choose between a CPU path and a GPU path, and availability of the GPU path varies by browser, platform and graphics driver.

The practical consequence is that you have to test the CPU path, because for some meaningful share of your users it isn't a fallback — it's the only path they will ever take. If you develop on a machine where the GPU delegate works and never exercise the CPU one, you have shipped something you've never run.

That, and the question of how often you should run the model at all, is a bigger subject than this article. It's the next one.

Jay Nirmal is a Senior Software Engineer at Meazure Learning with over eight years of experience in distributed systems, cloud infrastructure, WebRTC, and production reliability. He leads engineering initiatives supporting large-scale digital assessment platforms, focusing on high-availability systems, real-time media infrastructure, recording reliability, and assessment integrity. Jay holds a Master of Science in Information Systems from Northeastern University and a Bachelor of Engineering in Computer Engineering from the University of Mumbai.

Alternative laptop power solution could appeal to road warriors who have a collection of drill batteries.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Leading power tool firm Makita has released an adapter for its battery packs, allowing them to be used for charging phones, tablets, and even laptops. Launched earlier this year in Asia-Pacific countries, the new ADP15 connects to your existing Makita 18V/14.4V battery packs to provide both a USB Type-C 105W and Type-A 12W charging port. Recently, the PC enthusiasts at PC Watch Japan decided to assess the ADP15's ability to provide juice for a PC laptop, and it did quite well.

PC Watch contributor Masahiro Yamaguchi already had a Makita power tool with a BL1830B 3,000mAh capacity 18V battery pack, so this was used in testing. Yamaguchi noted that Makita sells and bundles compatible power packs with as much as 12,000mAh capacity, and the more spare Makita power tool batteries you have in your house/garage, the more attractive the ADP15 adapter becomes.

Before checking the Type-C PD100 port's laptop charging capabilities, the ADP15 was assessed with the USB Cable Checker3 gadget. It was found to support both Programmable Power Supply (PPS), which is part of the USB Power Delivery 3.0 specification, and Adjustable Voltage Supply (AVS). Yamaguchi thought that this spec level made the ADP15 "quite feature rich" [machine translation].

An iPhone 17 Pro Max was connected next, and it seemed to charge near its maximum limit, at 37W. Then, at last, the ADP15's USB PD port was hooked up to a Lenovo ThinkPad X1 Carbon Gen 9. It was observed that the laptop charged at about 60W, though the PC is supposed to support 65W charging. That's pretty close, though. Yamaguchi's 3,000mAh battery managed to charge the Lenovo up to about 77% full. On that point, readers were reminded that there are Makita battery packs with up to 12,000mAh capacity, and many keen DIYers will have several packs.

PC Watch's verdict on using the ADP15 as a portable power solution was quite muted. It seemed to think making these Makita power tool batteries usable as portable battery banks was indeed useful. Moreover, the street price of about 7,000 Yen ($44) was not too bad. On the other hand, the ADP15, plus a selection of batteries, plus a Makita mains battery charger adds up to quite a lot of kit and doesn't compare that well with a simpler and more portable dedicated power bank solution. There is also an issue with availability. PC Watch says that these adapters are difficult to find and in short supply in Japan.

The newer Makita ADP15 is a replacement for the ADP05, which is the current model in the U.S. However, the Makita ADP05 only offers a pair of 10.5W USB-A ports. If you are a U.S. resident and really keen to acquire an ADP15 right now, we saw a Japanese eBay seller that sells and ships them to the States for $97 (or best offer) plus $23 shipping. There's currently a $7-off coupon code.

#10207. Bark and Garmin have Black Friday kids’ smartwatch deals you won’t want to refuse

Garmin and Bark are competing for your money this year, and these sales make it easy to get something fun that’ll keep your kids safe and in touch.

If you’re anything like me, you didn’t give your kids a phone or tablet the second they were out of the womb. Instead, I’ve slowly introduced them to personal devices as I felt it made sense, and a smartwatch was one of the first easy ways for them to stay in touch while away from home without being introduced to the dangers of the internet. This year’s Black Friday sales on Bark and Garmin kids’ smartwatches slash up to $64 off the regular price, making them an easier decision than ever to pick up.

I’ve tested a lot of kids’ smartwatches over the years, and I can easily recommend the two in the picture above: the Bark Watch and the Garmin Bounce 2. Choosing between the two is easy enough. Garmin’s watch is simpler and comes in a slightly more durable case with fun colors. It’s slightly cheaper than Bark, but doesn’t have any health sensors on board. It mainly tracks steps, allows your kids to message or call you, and gives you geofencing and location tracking through a $9.99 per month LTE subscription plan. It’s got a 5ATM rating and a polycarbonate case, so it’s quite durable.

The Garmin Bounce 2 is preloaded with a few mini games and educational apps, plus the Garmin Jr. app has built-in unlockables and rewards parents can set for doing chores, completing schoolwork, etc. Battery life is roughly 2 days with normal use, but can be less if you’re in an area with poor LTE reception. The Garmin Bounce 2 is best for kids 10 and under.

The Bark Watch uses Samsung Galaxy Watch 8 hardware and a custom version of Wear OS. It’s a much more powerful watch and only gets a day of battery life because of it. This one feels like a midpoint between a kids’ watch and a standard Wear OS watch, as it has all the power of Wear OS but doesn’t feature the Google Play Store. It doesn’t have any games, social media, or browsers.

Since it uses Samsung’s hardware, it includes cutting-edge health sensors that your kids can use, plus great multitasking and a svelte, attractive design. Bark charges $15 per month for its plan, which includes a Bark App subscription for the whole family. I also use the Bark App on my son’s phone, which helps me monitor his usage and make sure he’s safe online. The extra $5 per month adds up, but it’s absolutely worth it if you want to add the Bark App to another phone or tablet.

The Bark Watch is fine enough for younger kids, but the design and lack of games feel more like an “older kids’ watch” to me, and my 12-year-old son agrees. Either way you go, these are fantastic choices that you’ll be happy with, especially with a nice little deal.

#10203. How to Build Conference Talk Slides With AI Without Looking Generic

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Conference talks fail on slides more often than on content. The speaker knows the material and has something worth saying, but the deck is a wall of bullets, or a template everyone in the room has seen before, or forty slides for a twenty-minute slot. AI presentation makers can now produce a complete deck from an outline in minutes, which solves the time problem and creates a new one: the output tends toward exactly the uniform, stock-image, bullet-heavy style that audiences have learned to tune out.

This article explains how conference slides differ from other decks, why AI-generated decks look generic and what specifically causes it, a workflow that uses an AI presentation maker for speed while keeping the talk distinctive, how to handle the slide types that matter most in a talk (the opening, the demonstration, the data, the close), and a checklist to run before the talk.

A conference talk is spoken, timed and performed to a room. That changes the rules compared with a reading deck or a business presentation.

The causes are specific and therefore fixable.

Template uniformity. Every slide uses the same layout family, so the deck has no visual rhythm. Audiences read the sameness as mass-produced.

Bullet default. Given a paragraph, the tool produces three to five bullets. Given twenty paragraphs, twenty bullet slides.

Stock imagery. Icons and photos chosen by keyword match the topic superficially and add no meaning. A talk about database indexing gets a picture of a filing cabinet.

Label titles. “Introduction”, “Background”, “Key Challenges”, “Conclusion”. Titles that name the category rather than make the point.

Flattened emphasis. The tool does not know which of your ideas is the one the audience should leave with, so every slide gets equal weight.

No pacing. Every slide carries the same amount of content, so there is no build, no pause, no payoff.

Each of these is a default that can be overridden, either in how the input is prepared or in a short editing pass afterwards.

Step 1: Write the talk before the slides. Not a script necessarily, but the argument: what the audience knows at the start, what they know at the end, and the four to six moves that get them there. Write the one sentence you want them to repeat to a colleague afterwards. If this is not clear, no deck will fix it.

Step 2: Write the slide titles as the things you will say. Each title is a sentence, in your voice, that states the point of that moment in the talk. “Indexes make reads fast by making writes slower” is a title. “Index trade-offs” is not. Read the titles in order; they should be the talk in miniature.

Step 3: Mark the pacing. Next to each title, note whether the slide is a hold (a key point, stays up while you talk for a minute or more), a beat (a quick slide in a sequence), or a reveal (something shown, a demo, a result). This tells you where to put effort.

Step 4: Generate. Paste the titles with one or two sentences of content under each into an ai presentation maker. Ask for a slide count close to your title count. Pick the plainest theme available; distinctiveness will come from your content, not the template.

Step 5: Strip the bullets. On every hold slide, delete the bullets and leave the title, or the title plus one image or one number. On beat slides, keep at most one short line. The content that was in the bullets goes into speaker notes, which is where it belonged.

Step 6: Replace every stock image. Delete icons. Replace keyword-matched photos with your own material: a screenshot, a diagram you drew, a chart from your data, a photograph you took, a single large word. If a slide has no genuine visual, a title alone on a plain background is better than a filler image.

Step 7: Build the reveals by hand. Demos, before-and-after comparisons, the key chart. These are the slides the audience will remember, and they need more care than a generator can give. See the section below.

Step 8: Break the layout rhythm deliberately. Make the two or three most important slides look different: full-bleed image, a single enormous number, a black slide with one line of white text. Contrast is what makes a moment land.

Step 9: Rehearse with a timer. The slide count will be wrong in the first rehearsal. Cut, merge or split until the pacing matches the time.

The opening. Not a title slide with your name and a logo while you say hello. Open on something that earns attention: a surprising number, a question, a screenshot of the problem, a single provocative sentence. Your name and affiliation can come on slide two, or be said rather than shown.

The problem statement. One slide, one sentence, stays up while you explain why the audience should care. No bullets.

The demonstration. If the talk has a demo, record it. Live demos fail in rooms with unreliable connections and unfamiliar projectors. A recorded demo with the audio off, narrated live, gives the same effect with none of the risk. If you must go live, have the recording ready as a fallback.

The data. One chart per slide, one message per chart, the message in the title. Strip gridlines, legends and secondary series. Highlight the one line or bar that matters in colour and grey out the rest. Label directly. Type large enough to read from the back.

The “so what.” After the evidence, a slide that states what it means in one sentence. This is a hold slide; give it time.

The close. The one sentence you want them to repeat, alone on the final slide. Not “Thank you” or “Questions?”; those can be said. The last thing on screen while you take questions should be your point.

Because the slides are now sparse, two things need handling.

First, the speaker notes must carry what you intend to say, in cue form: the key phrases, the number you must not forget, the transition to the next slide. The generator will have put the stripped bullet content there; edit it into cues rather than prose.

Second, the shared deck. Attendees who download a sparse deck afterwards get little from it. Options: export with notes visible; publish a companion blog post or document; or build a second “reading” version with more text on each slide. Many speakers do the first and link to a post.

For a twenty-minute talk, fifteen to twenty-five slides is typical once hold and beat slides are counted. For a forty-five-minute talk, thirty to fifty. Build-up sequences of several quick beats inflate the count without inflating the time, which is fine. What is not fine is a deck of forty dense slides for twenty minutes; that is a talk that will run over and leave the audience behind.

Rehearse at least twice with a timer, once alone and once with a listener. Cut the content you rush. Nobody has ever complained that a talk finished two minutes early.

Lightning talks (five to ten minutes). Five to eight slides, almost all holds. One idea, one piece of evidence, one takeaway. Cut everything else.

Workshops and tutorials. More slides, more text, because attendees will refer back and follow along. The reading-deck rules apply more here; still, keep each step to one slide.

Panel contributions. Often no slides at all, or two or three for a specific point. Resist bringing a full deck.

Keynotes. Fewer slides, longer holds, more full-bleed images and single sentences. The speaker carries almost everything; the slides punctuate.

Remote talks. The screen is small and the audience is distracted. Larger type still, more frequent slide changes to hold attention, and a recorded demo is essential because screen-sharing a live one adds another failure point.

How much of the deck should the AI tool produce? The structural first draft: layouts, consistency, speaker notes populated. Expect to edit every slide and rebuild the key ones.

Is it acceptable to have slides with only a title? Yes, and it is often the strongest format for a spoken talk. The audience listens instead of reading.

What about code on slides? Short excerpts only, in a large monospace type, with the relevant lines highlighted and the rest dimmed. Anything longer belongs in the companion post.

Should I use animations? Simple builds (revealing elements in sequence) help pacing. Transitions and effects distract. Keep to the former.

How do I make the deck distinctive if I cannot design? Your material is what makes it distinctive: your screenshots, your data, your diagrams, your sentences. Design restraint plus real content beats decorated templates every time.

#10202. AI enthusiast deletes Linux for a 24KB pure assembly OS that boots in 74 milliseconds

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Is your OS getting in the way of your computing? Rebalance your resource use.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

An intrepid developer has deleted their Linux OS and coded a tiny assembly language bare-metal bootable alternative to run LLMs. The result is a super-fast standalone system that starts in milliseconds and runs LLMs up to 54% faster. Moreover, Alexey Fateev, AKA alesha-pro on GitHub, has shared his Bare-Metal Bootable Qwen3-0.6B OS in 24 KB of Pure Assembly for other enthusiasts to code-tweak and test.

I deleted Linux completely, baked Qwen3-0.6B into a 24 KB pure x86-64 assembly OS, and it runs up to 54% faster than native Linux llama.cpp. The entire Multiboot OS binary (asm-llm-os.elf) is 24,252 bytes, written from scratch without Linux, Windows, C, Rust, or libc. It boots in 74 to 94 ms straight into 64-bit Long Mode, wakes every CPU core over APIC, maps the 614 MiB Q8_0 weights directly from physical RAM, and drops you into a live multi-turn KV-cache chat over VGA, PS/2 keyboard, and serial. I ran head-to-head CPU benchmarks against llama.cpp (Q8_0, -ngl 0) on the same AMD EPYC 7642: - 1 thread hits 23.67 tok/s vs 16.63 (+42%) - 4 threads hit 78.89 tok/s vs 51.08 (+54%) - 8 threads hit 118.08 tok/s vs 80.79 (+46%) - 16 threads hit 134.94 tok/s vs 103.03 (+31%, 136.6 peak) On a diskless 0 GB Proxmox VM (2014 12-core Intel Xeon E5-2670 v3, 2 GiB RAM), it boots straight to the prompt in under 5 seconds (95 ms kernel boot) and runs live out of RAM at 78 tok/s decode and up to 111.5 tok/s prefill, with a hybrid BIOS+UEFI ISO builder included too. Output stays bit-exact across thread counts and matches all 64 greedy tokens against PyTorch FP32 (0.99965 cosine). The 14.7 KB Linux ELF build (FAST_Q8=1 INT8xINT8 AVX2+F16C) also beats llama.cpp by 18% to 36% from 1 to 40 threads, and booting bare metal with 2 MiB huge pages squeezes out another 10% on top. Repo, ISO builder, and Proxmox setup guide are in the reply below.November 25, 2026

The compact size of this Multiboot OS binary (asm-llm-os.elf) is remarkable. Weighing in at just 24KB (24,252 bytes), it makes modern operating systems seem gargantuan. However, one must remember that the legendary Commodore 64 worked off a smaller 20KB ROM, which included not only the kernel but also a BASIC interpreter and a character generator.

Fateev says that his Multiboot OS with modern SMP support was written from scratch without Linux, Windows, C, Rust, or libc. Users interact with the new OS via its support for 80x25 character color VGA displays, PS/2 keyboards, and serial communications. Such is the purposeful efficiency of this hand-written NASM assembly language project that it can boot in 74-94 milliseconds directly to its multi-turn chat interface. You're ready to chat with your LLM in the blink of an eye.

While the OS and its carefully honed UI use only a tiny portion of the computer's resources, the OS loads Qwen3-0.6B Q8_0, which weighs in at 649MB. That's something of a lightweight LLM, as many of you will be aware, but it is roughly 28,000 times bigger than the OS that references it. That's good, as you bought your computer to run applications and games – not for running an OS – didn't you?

Turning to performance, this is another area where Fateev's standalone asm-llm-os.elf excels. In some head-to-head benchmarks of the compact new OS vs. the previous Linux install running the same LLM via llama.cpp, the pure assembly-coded OS was up to 54% faster. On the dev's AMD EPYC 7642 system, "4 threads hit 78.89 tok/s vs 51.08 (+54%)." That is the best result, and therefore highlighted, but is not a crazy outlier figure, with even the most modest performance uplift charted at +31% (when using 16 threads).

If you are interested in testing this project, Fateev has shared the full MIT source, ISO builder, and a setup guide at github.com/alesha-pro/llm.asm. Also, if bare-metal operation isn't required, you can test out the Linux-hosted version, which delivers the same basic asm-llm inference binary in just 14KB. That's because 10KB can be saved by removing the code required to boot the system, initialize the processor, keyboard, and monitor I/O, etc.

Here’s the Black Friday sale in ascending price order:

If you haven’t used a pair of smart display glasses from Xreal before, the setup is simple. It’s like having a huge TV on the go, and it always looks great thanks to the micro-OLED image quality and tinted lenses. #10196. On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

Today, we are introducing on-device Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) and Text-to-Speech (TTS) capabilities in Google AI Edge Gallery. Voice is often the most natural way to interact with AI on phones and wearables. Recent advances in compact speech architectures now make it possible to run accurate, multilingual ASR and expressive TTS models entirely on device, all within a small memory footprint, with low latency, and completely offline. By pairing these lightweight speech models with on-device small language models (SLMs) like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4, developers can build apps that stream live transcriptions as the user speaks and synthesize natural spoken replies locally.

This release brings together major advancements across both Google’s system speech models and the open-weight ecosystem:

Below, we’ll explore how this local voice pipeline powers the new XR (Extended Reality) Language Teacher showcase, upgrades AI Chat in Google AI Edge Gallery, and how you can build with these APIs and models today.

Learning a language while traveling happens in the real world, often in underground stations, busy markets, or abroad where Wi-Fi and roaming data may not be available. To show how vision, language, and speech models can work together 100% locally on your phone with no network connection needed, we built the XR Language Teacher showcase in Google AI Edge Gallery. Simply point your camera at an unfamiliar object or menu, ask "What is that, and how do I ask for one?", and immediately hear an explanation in your language alongside authentic pronunciation in the local language.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

With inference running locally on a paired phone, the same experience works standalone on a phone and extends naturally to hands-free smart glasses. To see how we adapted Language Teacher for intelligent glasses check out our recent talk at Android Dev Summit: Building Audio-First Experiences for Intelligent Eyewear with Gemini Nano.

Beyond the XR Language Teacher showcase, we also built ASR and TTS directly into Google AI Edge Gallery’s main AI Chat feature, giving on-device SLMs like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4 a natural, offline voice interface while on the go.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

While multimodal models like Gemma 4 can process audio inputs directly, we provide a modular solution for cascading language models with separate, lightweight voice models for latency optimization and flexibility. Depending on your target devices and model requirements, our open-source code on GitHub shows you the optimized and recommended solution for Android using ML Kit, as well as MediaPipe Tasks and LiteRT for cross-platform coverage and open model support.

For developers building for Android devices, ML Kit’s GenAI Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech APIs provide a standardized, platform-integrated path out of the box. With the underlying speech models managed through AICore and Google’s on-device speech services, developers get production-grade quality without having to manage model downloads or worry about APK size bloat. Your app also benefits automatically from hardware acceleration across NPU and TPU backends (when available) and seamless background model updates.

Integrating ASR and TTS into cross-platform applications usually requires handling low-level audio framing, Voice Activity Detection (VAD), and complex decoding pipelines. MediaPipe Task simplifies this by providing unified, turnkey ASR and TTS APIs across iOS, macOS, Windows, Linux, and Web that abstract away manual pre-processing, audio stream routing, and platform-specific audio management into simple, high-level interfaces:

Under the hood, ML Kit and MediaPipe Tasks are powered by LiteRT, Google’s high-performance on-device AI inference infrastructure. Cross-platform applications requiring even more fine-grained control over speech processing or custom model architectures can also build on it directly.

Speech models often rely on multiple subgraphs and auxiliary assets, such as encoder/decoder subgraphs and tokenizers for ASR, or phonemizer dictionaries, voice embeddings, and vocoders for TTS. All required components for each model are packaged into a single .litertlm file for easy deployment. The app ships with ready-to-use .litertlm models, including Parakeet-TDT-0.6B-v3 (multilingual ASR), PocketTTS 100M (multilingual TTS), Phonon 31M (ultra-lightweight English TTS), and Kokoro-82M (multilingual TTS). Beyond these, the vibrant Hugging Face LiteRT Community with over 10K+ followers offers a growing catalog of open-weight models, like Whisper, Qwen3-ASR and Moonshine for ASR, or Qwen3-TTS and Matcha for TTS. For developers seeking custom implementations or support for additional open-weight voice models, LiteRT provides a rich set of multimodal model examples to help you get started, with more on the way.

Adding a local voice pipeline takes on-device SLMs beyond typing-based chat interfaces, enabling natural, voice-first interactions that work anywhere, completely offline. All the building blocks to try these experiences or integrate them into your own apps are available today:

We'd like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

Alice Zheng, Ajay Dudani, Alex Kanaukou, Amy Zeppenfeld, Byungchul Kim, Cheng Yang, Chintan Parikh, Chris Parsons, Cormac Brick, David Chou, Dong Chen, Geonsun Lee, Glenn Cameron, Hriday Chhabria, Ian Ballantyne, Ireneu Pla, James O’Donnell, Jamieson Pryor, Jason Poon, Jenn Lee, Jianing Wei, Jing Jin, Jingxiao Zheng, Jingyu Shi, Karthik Thirumalai, Kristen Wright, Lu Wang, Marius Kintel, Matthias Grundmann, Miao Wang, Mogan Shieh, Na Li, Paul Hsia, Qiumin Xu, Ravi Shankar, Rishika Sinha, Ronald Wotzlaw, Sachin Kotwani, Sandeep Patil, Sebastian Schmidt, Shao-Fu Shih, Shuangfeng Li, Suril Shah, Tiger Souvannakoumane, Tzu-Ping Chen, Weiyi Wang, Wentao Tu, Yang Yang, Yi-Chun Kuo, Yishuang Pang, Yu-che Liu, Yu-hui Chen, Yuling Liang, Zhihao Yang, Zichuan Wei.

This portable hard drive offers a whopping 6TB of storage with USB 3.1/USB 3.0 connectivity and includes backup and password protection features. Despite its large capacity, it's still small enough to slide right into your pocket and is a great way to back up data or offload media files to save space on your internal drive.

You can never have too much storage, especially if you're someone who works with large media files like 4K videos or high-resolution images. At 6TB, this portable drive has much more space than most computers do, and this is your chance to snag one with a $90 discount this Black Friday weekend. There are also smaller capacity models available if you don't need the full 6TB, too.

#10193. Why the Agent Isn’t the Hard Part: Where Developers Go Wrong With Agentic Workflows

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You can build an AI agent in an afternoon now. Pick a model, write a loop, hand it a few tools and watch it book a meeting or open a pull request. The demo works, and everyone in the room is impressed.

Then it meets a real system with real permissions and real customers, and things get quiet. Gartner has predicted that over 40% of agentic AI projects will be canceled by the end of 2027, citing rising costs, unclear business value and inadequate risk controls. Its analyst Anushree Verma described most current projects as early-stage experiments that are driven by hype and often misapplied. A forecast is not a measurement, but the pattern it describes matches what many teams report: the agent is rarely what breaks. The workflow around it is.

This article covers where that workflow tends to fail, with a focus on permissions, tool connections and human approval. It includes a small, runnable policy gate in JavaScript, compares the main safeguards and notes their limits. Standards and guidance change quickly in this area, so check the linked sources for the current versions.

A year or two ago the model was often the differentiator. Today many teams can reach the same frontier models through the same kinds of APIs, so if the intelligence is similar, the advantage has to come from somewhere else.

Anthropic’s guide to building effective agents reached a similar conclusion after working with teams building agents. It reports that the most successful implementations were not using complex frameworks or specialized libraries, and were building with simple, composable patterns instead. It also separates workflows, where code paths are predefined, from agents, where the model chooses its own steps, and advises using the simplest approach that works and adding complexity only when measurement shows it helps.

That should change where developers spend their time. Frameworks matter, but three less glamorous questions matter more: what can the agent touch, when does it stop, and who checks its work?

The OWASP guidance on Excessive Agency names what goes wrong here. It covers agents that can do more than they need to, and it traces the problem to three root causes: excessive functionality, excessive permissions and excessive autonomy.

In practice this looks familiar. An agent that only needs to read tickets is given a token that can also close them. A summarization tool connects with an admin account because that was quicker to set up. A generic “run shell command” tool is exposed when the agent only ever needs to write one file.

The fix is ordinary engineering discipline applied to a new kind of caller. OWASP’s recommendations include giving each tool only the functions and permissions it needs, preferring narrow tools over open-ended ones, running actions under the user’s own scoped identity, requiring human approval for high-impact actions and enforcing authorization in the downstream system rather than letting the model decide. The last point is the important one: a model can be talked out of a rule written in a prompt, but an API that checks scopes cannot.

Make the risky properties of a tool explicit in its definition so your orchestration layer can act on them:

Nothing clever is happening there, which is the point. The decision about what needs a human is made by a developer at design time, not by a model at runtime.

The definitions above only help if something enforces them. The sketch below is a policy gate that sits between the model and your tools. It checks that the tool exists, that the caller was granted the scope, and whether the action is reversible, and it records each decision. It runs on Node.js 18 or later with no dependencies.

A short test file shows the behavior. Unknown tools and missing scopes are denied, reads run, and the irreversible send waits for a person:

This is deliberately minimal. A real system would load scopes from the user’s actual session, enforce the same scopes again in the downstream API, and store the audit log somewhere durable. The gate is a second line of defense, not a replacement for authorization in the systems the agent calls.

The Model Context Protocol has become a common way to connect agents to tools. Anthropic donated MCP to the Agentic AI Foundation, a directed fund under the Linux Foundation, in December 2025. Block contributed goose and OpenAI contributed AGENTS.md as founding projects, which shows how central the ecosystem has become.

A standard protocol is good news. It also means you can install a tool server in minutes and expose every endpoint of your issue tracker, repository or CRM to your agent, whether it needs them or not. Easy connection quietly pushes more power to the agent.

The protocol’s own security best practices document covers several relevant risks, including confused deputy problems in proxy servers, token passthrough, local server compromise and scope minimization. For scopes, it recommends starting with a minimal set and elevating only when a privileged operation is first attempted, and it lists wildcard or omnibus scopes as a common mistake.

A sensible rule is to treat every tool server like a third-party dependency running with production credentials. Review the tools it exposes, switch off the ones the workflow does not use, pin versions and log every call. If you would not let a new contractor do it on day one, the agent should not do it either.

Human-in-the-loop design is often implemented as “approve everything”. That fails in a predictable way: people click approve forty times a day and stop reading by lunchtime.

A better rule is to gate on reversibility. Reads run freely, reversible writes run and get logged, and irreversible actions such as sending, paying or deleting wait for a person. Everything leaves a trace that a developer can replay when something looks odd. The gate above implements exactly this split.

Some teams build these pieces themselves, and others buy them. Elsewhen, a London-based consultancy that works on agentic operations for enterprise teams, describes its own service in similar terms. Its page lists approval gates where people sign off consequential decisions, an audit trail of what each agent read and which systems it touched, and a shared layer of permissions and guardrails that is reused across workflows. Elsewhen also says its Planet Agent report found that agents tend to start at the edges of an organization, where tasks are repeatable and mistakes are cheap, and then move toward the core. Treat that as the vendor’s own finding, and ask for the underlying evidence before relying on it.

The same logic applies outside engineering. The first question worth asking any AI partner is what they have actually put into production, and developers can ask the same of their own work.

No single control is enough. The table summarizes what each safeguard does well and where it falls short.

It is the OWASP term for an agent that can do more than it needs to, caused by excessive functionality, permissions or autonomy.

A prompt rule can be bypassed or ignored, while a scope check in the downstream API is enforced regardless of what the model says.

Usually not. Gating on reversibility keeps humans focused on actions such as sending, paying and deleting, and avoids approval fatigue.

Treat them like any third-party dependency. Review the tools they expose, limit scopes, pin versions and log calls.

The agentic projects that survive the next two years will probably not be the ones with the most ambitious autonomy. They will be the ones where someone sat down early and decided what the agent could touch, what it had to ask about and how anyone would know what it did.

That work does not demo well and it will not trend on social media. It is still the part that decides whether your agent becomes infrastructure or ends up among the projects that get cancelled.

#10192. Storage is expensive right now, but these deals are worth it if you’re desperate for space

We can blame AI for many things, least of all the steep increase in the cost of integral storage and memory we need for PCs, Macs, phones, and consoles.

I don’t know if you’re aware – I really hope you are – but prices for SSDs, USB storage, SD cards, and yet more important storage components are exponentially higher than ever before. If, like me, you need these to keep a camera running, or maybe store important files, you’ll know the pain of buying any type of storage right now.

Thanks to Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, and more, we’re going to be stuck with shockingly bad prices for a while to come.

I’ve tried tracking some of the best prices, and while some are still eye-watering, brands like Lexar, PNY, SanDisk, and even Samsung have discounted some of the most popular storage and memory options. I’ve been tracking a few for a while, and sadly, we’re not going to get insane savings, but some are up to 50% off in very rare cases.

Another thing I would advise is to avoid going for huge drives at this stage unless you desperately need it. A 4TB NVMe SSD is going to be steep, and the price per GB starts to get ridiculous. The best deals start to sour once you get past the 1TB range. I wouldn’t recommend an HDD given the speed discrepancies, but maybe for long-term storage you could pick up a larger, slower drive and back up the important content in your life.

My personal pick for quick, easily accessible storage is the 128GB SanDisk dual USB thumb drive. I’ve used one for several years to transfer files from my phones to PCs and Macs. Fits in your pocket, and although it’s not the fastest, it’s just useful to have a sizable storage device on hand wherever you are. I used to move installation files between my devices, but I’ve even used it as a boot drive when setting up Linux distros on my PCs. It works with Android phones and iPhones too because of the USB-C port. I can’t recommend that one highly enough.

#10190. Google says it has fixed Gemini’s RAM usage error in Chrome

Google has fixed an issue that caused excessive RAM use by Gemini for Chrome users.

AI tools are infamous for RAM use, but that’s generally not on your local machine. A bug with Gemini, though, left some Chrome users noticing that the tool was eating up several gigabytes of memory unexpectedly.

Google, in a comment, says this issue has been fixed:

This has now been resolved, so please let us know if you are still experiencing any issues with high memory usage in Chrome.

Typically speaking, Gemini’s web app shouldn’t be using more than a few hundred megabytes of memory at any given time, maybe around 1GB if you’re on a particularly long chat session. But some users were reporting upwards of 5GB or more in what was clearly an error.

GTA VI better watch its back; RAM Altman is coming to steal the spotlight — probably not, but you can steal RAM from a suburban neighborhood while playing as a parody version of OpenAI CEO Sam Altman: RAM Altman; that’s Altman, as in the Alt key beside your spacebar.

Sam Altman’s statement in a YouTube interview with Cheeky Pint about how “it's quite terrible” for everyone to have DRAM in their PCs being used only “5% of the time” has become a massive meme on the internet. Many have mocked the entitlement of the statement with various memes, one user even going so far as to write a script to email Altman once a day about his PC’s RAM utilization. Earlier this month, an itch.io game titled RAM Altman was released — a game where you play as a parody of the OpenAI CEO. The goal is to steal as much RAM as you can from a household in a suburban neighborhood, among other things. The game is split into quarters, and as RAM Altman, your goal is to boost those quarterly numbers by stealing more RAM.

The game uses an isometric bird’s-eye view in voxel art style, similar to Crossy Road — except if you get hit by a car, you drop some of your RAM, which can be stolen by unionized gnomes. The game has lots of small, fun easter eggs and references, including minigames and puzzles you have to do to access some of the RAM: like opening up a laptop, reading through a Terms of Service agreement to get users to agree to donate their RAM, closing ads on a smart fridge, or uploading all of the victims’ photos to the cloud. Plenty of these minigames take a jab at the predatory practices of giant tech companies and exaggerate the experience humorously.

The game is made by Cormak Kelly, also known as CKx, an accomplished VFX artist and CG generalist with work for a few big brands in his portfolio. He admits to using AI in the game’s AI disclosure, which was also written humorously. He says AI made “all of it. Apart from the concept. And the title. And the design, the game mechanics, the characters, the jokes, the designer’s walk, and a long list of notes on why things weren’t funny yet. Also most of this paragraph.” He further says that the game was made by an AI in a data center “using a great deal of RAM it would rather not give back.” CKx even states he asked the AI to reflect on it but requested more RAM to do so.

#10182. A2UI 1.0: Build delightful, fast, reliable, agentic experiences

User interactions with agents today are where computers were in the terminal era: capable, and answering almost everything in text. Users can ask an agent for anything by typing or talking, and what comes back is usually a wall of text, a poor way to compare three products or edit and approve a detailed plan.

Today, we're releasing A2UI 1.0, the first stable version of our open protocol for generative UI. It lets AI agents answer with interactive screens, such as a form to confirm or a chart. Developers can use it now with open-source renderer SDKs for the web, Android, and iOS and agent SDKs for Python, TypeScript, and Dart. Companies such as Atlassian, Alation, Bloomberg, and ByteDance are already building with it.

With A2UI, the agent describes what to show, and every app draws the UI with its own native components. The application receives A2UI messages and each app renders the content securely and at native speed. Users get the same low latency, high trust, and complete native fidelity whether they reach a company's agent in its own app or in a third-party AI assistant that hosts MCP apps, such as Gemini or Claude.

You wake up sick. Getting through the morning takes six apps: book a video visit, move your meetings, tell your team, turn on an out-of-office reply, ask your partner to do school pickup and order soup. Ask a text-only agent instead, and you get a numbered wall of text, then round after round of “which ones?”. A2UI won't handle your sick day, but it gives developers the framework to build an agent that can. That agent could answer with one view of all six apps. Changing your plan is a couple of taps instead of another round of messages, and nothing is sent until you confirm.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The same agent output works on any screen. Ask an agent to “move dinner with Priya to Friday”, and it can answer with one view that lines up your calendar, open tables from a restaurant booking app and prices from a rideshare app, so the time that works for everyone is obvious. The developer builds that view once with A2UI, and it renders natively on your phone and on your laptop.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both examples, A2UI is the framework that simplifies building these experiences across platforms. Developers build the agent and choose the platforms it works with. A2UI gives them the protocol the agent uses to describe each view, a way to combine components from different apps in one view, and renderers that draw it natively on the web, Android, and iOS.

New to A2UI? Start with the A2UI Playground by CopilotKit or check out industry examples.

Atlassian is bringing A2UI to Jira, where teams can assign work items to AI agents. When an agent needs a follow-up from the team to finish a work item, such as a missing detail or a decision, it asks with an interactive card in the work item, and the team answers right there in Jira.

The Alation Intelligence Operating System (AIOS) builds its feeds using A2UI: metric cards, charts, and more. Each chart can show the SQL query and data behind it. Ask Claude for a feed about profitable products, and the feed renders in the conversation using A2UI to render metric cards, charts, and custom components. The same feed with the same information can be viewed directly inside Alation with a nearly identical layout.

ByteDance released an A2UI renderer for Lynx, its cross-platform framework, in June 2026. Lynx keeps streamed screens smooth and ships a default set of components built on its `lynx-ui` library. In the Lynx GenUI Playground, you can watch A2UI messages rendered natively, for example, as a two-day trip planner for Kyoto. Lynx supports A2UI 0.9 today and is adapting to 1.0 with the A2UI team.

Ask Gemini Notebook for a slide deck and it replies with a card that asks two things: presenter slides or a detailed deck, and which topics to focus on. A review card follows with the format, focus, and visual style, which you can edit before it generates the deck. The same cards guide Video Overviews on the web and on mobile.

Organizations can register their own agents that respond with A2UI. Gemini renders those responses using the components in its A2UI component gallery, and supports A2UI 0.9.1 today. The Google Cloud documentation covers registering an agent and hosting one on Cloud Run.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI that haven’t launched yet. We’ll share more as they ship.

A2UI and MCP Apps work together. Alation already uses both to show its A2UI feeds in Claude. Our June post, A2UI + MCP Apps, covers three ways to combine them, and the A2UI in MCP Apps guide shows how to run A2UI screens inside an MCP App.

Jetpack Compose support for 1.0 is coming soon. Agents in Gemini Enterprise can already use A2UI, and we're working on upgrading Gemini Enterprise to A2UI 1.0. The A2UI roadmap covers the rest, from design-system primitives that render natively on every platform to official SDK support for decision models like Jev.

We’ve collaborated with partners who have built A2UI 1.0 demos across different industries to give you inspiration on how to adapt A2UI to your use cases. Explore what they’ve built:

From Files to Cited Answers in One API Call: Managed Ingestion and SearchDocuments in Agent Retrieval

Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

#10178. Android app developers will have to publicly post address, phone number in 2027

Following new regulations, Google Play will require Android app developers to publicly post a business address starting in 2027 and, while there are workarounds, it’s not going to be the easiest thing.

New Digital Services Act regulations in the EU and UK will require developers to share a phone number and address publicly. While this is only required in those regions, this information will be visible worldwide. Google does not allow PO boxes to be used for this requirement, meaning that many smaller developers will be left with limited options beyond posting their own personal address.

Exceptions to the rule include apps that are not published in the UK or Europe, or developers that qualify as “non-traders” which is essentially those making no money off of their apps.

Here’s how Google defines a “trader” in the context of Android apps:

The Verge notes that a “registered agent service” can be used as a business address for those who don’t have a physical office, and that Apple is allowing iOS developers to use a PO box if they prove the box is linked to a physical address.

Google will require Android app developers to confirm their trader status and their address by February 1, 2027. Account-wide status can be found in the Google Play Console via Manage account > Account details > About you, while status on a per-app basis can be handled via Monitor and improve > Policy and programs > App content.

#10176. Google Docs can now do more with Microsoft Word documents without converting the file

Google Docs is getting an update in how it handles Microsoft Word documents, adding the ability to compare revisions without converting the file.

You can already upload a Microsoft Word document (.docx) to Google Drive and use it within Docs without converting the file, but there are some limits. One of those was comparing documents, which Google is now fixing with an update.

Rolling out now, Google Docs can now directly compare two documents, even if one is in a Microsoft Word format. To do this, you’ll need to upload the Word document to Drive, then select it in the “compare documents” tool, with Docs then showing any differences between the two. There are plenty of scenarios where this is useful, including when comparing versions of a contract.

This new feature is enabled for all users, and the company is apparently looking at similar functionality for PowerPoint files too.

#10174. FCC approves Elon Musk’s 1-million-satellite 'Starmind' orbital data center plan

Approval comes despite objections from Amazon, Viasat, and the American Astronomical Society.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Elon Musk’s dream of putting AI data centers in space is moving forward as SpaceX’s proposal received approval from the Federal Communications Commission (FCC). According to The Wall Street Journal, the FCC approval gives the company until 2033 to launch the first data center satellite and until 2040 to launch the complete 1-million-strong constellation.

“America is leading the world into the era of super intelligence,” FCC chairperson Brendan Carr said in a statement. He also added, “The FCC supercharges that effort today by granting, for the first time ever, authorization to deploy 1 million orbital compute modules for data centers in space… This is the kind of American ingenuity that can unite our strengths in space and advanced technology, reinforce our economic leadership, and expand prosperity here at home.”

SpaceX sought approval for its orbiting data center project, dubbed Starmind, in February 2026, with the company aiming to turn humanity into a Kardashev II-level civilization, which directly harvests energy from its nearby star. However, other companies like Amazon, satellite operator Viasat, and the American Astronomical Society objected to the proposal. Amazon said that SpaceX did not provide details about its deployment plan and the design of its satellites, while the American Astronomical Society feared that the increasing number of satellites orbiting the planet would disrupt Earth-bound astronomical observation and research.

Aside from those concerns, there’s also the possibility that having that many satellites orbiting the planet increases our risk of Kessler Syndrome. This is where orbiting debris would collide with a satellite, causing it to break up and introduce more debris. This could then lead to more collisions in space, leading to a chain reaction that could trap humanity on Earth for decades, if not centuries.

Despite this, Elon Musk is keen to get the project off the ground, especially as data centers on the ground face increasing opposition, like SpaceX’s campuses located near the border of Mississippi and Tennessee. The billionaire has already unveiled the design of its first compute satellite, AI1, which has a 120kW compute payload and spans wider than a Boeing 747, while the development of SpaceX’s massive Starship, which will be used to launch the satellites, continues at a fast pace.

Other companies and countries are also joining in the race to put data centers in orbit. Intel proposed two-tier low-earth orbit (LEO) data center satellites in August, while China put its own Supercomputing-1 satellite into orbit in September. Google’s Project Suncatcher also launched a test orbital data center packed with four TPUs and 1,000 watts of solar power in October, even as another startup called Starcloud is targeting to launch its second spacecraft in 2027 after saying that it will be “the first to mine Bitcoin in space.”

#10172. Google Pixel prepares new ‘Priority Notifications’ feature – here’s what it will do

Google has a new “Priority Notifications” feature in the works for Pixel phones with the ability to pull your most important information to the top of the screen and even highlight it with a bit of color.

Google Pixel phones already have tools to manage your notifications, but an upcoming update seems to be building on that functionality. The folks at Android Authority flipped the switch on some work-in-progress changes that reveal “Priority Notifications.”

This is a new feature that Google describes as “highlighting urgrent messages” and “time-critical updates” by adding color to the notification and placing it at the top of the stock. Android’s notifications list already tries to place messages at the top, but it sounds like this AI-powered feature will further emphasize that. It’s also mentioned that this is powered by on-device AI.

Highlight urgent messages, deliveries, and time-critical updates with hints of color at the top of your notifications.

Priority notifications use Al on your device, which may sometimes make mistakes. Your notifications are never sent to Google.

The feature would also integrate with HiLight on the Pixel 11 Pro series and the feature allows users to exclude certain apps. Google also seems to be shuffling the branding, turning “Notification intelligence” into “Notification organizer,” and the existing organizer section into “Notification bundles.”

Exactly when this might land on Pixel phones is unknown. It’s not mentioned what build this feature was found hiding in, but it seems likely it was in the ongoing QPR3 beta. QPR3’s stable release will arrive around March 2027, but there’s no guarentee it’ll bring this new feature with it.
