#10171. A Practical Guide to Browser Developer Tools for Debugging and Improving Websites

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Building a website involves more than writing HTML, CSS, and JavaScript. Developers also need to test how their code behaves in different browsers, identify layout problems, investigate JavaScript errors, and understand why certain pages take longer to load.

Browser developer tools make these tasks easier. They provide a way to inspect the code currently running in a browser without constantly changing files or guessing what might be wrong.

Whether you're developing a new website or maintaining an existing application, learning to use these tools can improve your debugging workflow and help you write more reliable code.

Let's explore some useful browser development tools and how to apply them to everyday web development tasks.

Browser developer tools, commonly called DevTools, are built-in utilities available in modern browsers such as Google Chrome, Mozilla Firefox, Microsoft Edge, and Safari.

They allow developers to examine a webpage's HTML structure, modify CSS properties, debug JavaScript, inspect network requests, and test responsive layouts.

You can usually open them by right-clicking a webpage and selecting Inspect. On Windows and Linux, the F12 key or Ctrl + Shift + I shortcut also opens DevTools in most browsers.

The following table highlights some commonly used features:

Understanding what each panel does makes it easier to select the right tool for a particular problem.

The Elements panel is one of the most frequently used features in browser DevTools.

It displays the Document Object Model (DOM), which represents the structure of the webpage currently loaded in the browser. Developers can select individual elements and examine the CSS rules affecting their appearance.

For example, imagine that a heading has more spacing than expected. Instead of immediately editing the stylesheet, inspect the heading and review its computed styles.

You might discover that the spacing comes from a margin inherited through another CSS rule or from a conflicting selector.

You can also experiment with CSS properties directly inside DevTools.

If the card appears too wide, you can temporarily change its width or padding in the Styles panel.

The browser immediately displays the result, allowing you to compare different values without repeatedly editing and saving your stylesheet.

However, these temporary changes do not automatically update your original CSS files. Once you've identified the correct solution, apply it to your project source code.

For more detailed CSS debugging techniques, developers can also read SitePoint's guide on CSS Debugging and Optimization: Browser-based Developer Tools.

JavaScript errors can prevent interactive features from working correctly. A navigation menu might stop opening, a form might fail to submit, or a button might not trigger the expected action.

The Console panel helps developers investigate these problems.

For example, consider the following JavaScript:

If the page does not contain an element with the ID submitButton, the selected value will be null. Calling addEventListener() on that value causes an error.

You can prevent this particular issue by checking whether the element exists:

The Console panel also allows developers to execute JavaScript expressions manually.

This returns the number of image elements currently present in the document.

You can use similar expressions to inspect elements, check values, and test small pieces of functionality.

Sometimes a JavaScript error is not immediately visible. A function may execute successfully but produce an unexpected result.

In such cases, the Sources panel in Chrome DevTools or the Debugger panel in Firefox can help.

A breakpoint pauses JavaScript execution at a specific line. Developers can then inspect variable values, examine the call stack, and execute the code step by step.

If the application displays an incorrect total, you can place a breakpoint inside calculateTotal().

When the function executes, the browser pauses and allows you to inspect the values of price, quantity, and total.

This is more useful than repeatedly adding console.log() statements throughout a large application.

Breakpoints are particularly helpful when debugging complex functions, asynchronous operations, and event handlers.

A webpage may contain several resources, including images, stylesheets, JavaScript files, fonts, and API requests.

If one of these resources fails to load, part of the website may stop working correctly.

The Network panel provides information about requests made by the browser.

It displays request URLs, HTTP status codes, response sizes, loading times, and other useful details.

For example, the following table explains some common HTTP responses developers may encounter:

Suppose an image is missing from a product page. Open the Network panel, reload the page, and filter the requests by images.

If the image returns a 404 response, check whether the file path is correct and whether the resource exists on the server.

The same process can help investigate failed API requests.

For example, if a frontend application requests product information from an API, the Network panel allows you to inspect the request method, response status, and returned data.

This helps developers distinguish between frontend problems and backend errors.

A website that looks correct on a desktop computer may not work properly on a smartphone.

Text can overflow, navigation menus can become difficult to use, and images may extend beyond their containers.

Browser DevTools include responsive design and device emulation features that allow developers to test different viewport sizes.

In Chrome, you can enable the device toolbar from the DevTools interface. Firefox provides a similar feature called Responsive Design Mode.

The desktop layout displays three columns, while smaller screens display one column.

You can test this behavior by changing the viewport width inside DevTools.

A useful responsive testing process includes:

Checking common mobile and tablet widths.

Testing both portrait and landscape orientations.

Checking whether navigation remains usable.

Verifying that buttons and links are easy to interact with.

Device emulation is useful during development, but it does not completely replace testing on physical devices.

A website can have correct HTML, CSS, and JavaScript but still provide a poor experience if it loads slowly or performs unnecessary work.

The Performance panel helps developers investigate browser activity over time.

It can record page interactions and provide information about scripting, rendering, painting, and other browser operations.

For example, a page might become unresponsive when a user opens a navigation menu. A performance recording can help identify whether a long-running JavaScript function is blocking the main thread.

Developers can also use tools such as Lighthouse to audit performance, accessibility, SEO, and other aspects of a webpage.

Instead of making random optimization changes, use the available measurements to identify the actual problem.

Some useful areas to investigate include:

Performance analysis should be repeated after making changes to determine whether the optimization actually improves the result.

These features are useful, but they can also introduce debugging challenges.

For example, an application might continue displaying outdated information because an old value remains stored in local storage.

The Application panel in Chrome DevTools allows developers to inspect and manage several types of browser storage.

If the application behaves unexpectedly because of stored data, inspecting local storage can help identify the problem.

Be careful when clearing storage because doing so may remove authentication sessions, saved preferences, or other application data.

Knowing individual DevTools features is useful, but developers benefit even more from using them in a consistent order.

The following workflow can make troubleshooting more efficient:

This process helps developers identify the cause of a problem before changing multiple parts of the application.

It also makes debugging easier when working with other developers because the issue can be reproduced and explained using specific observations.

Browser developer tools are essential for modern web development. They help developers inspect HTML and CSS, debug JavaScript, analyze network requests, test responsive layouts, and investigate performance issues.

The most effective approach is to select the appropriate tool for the problem instead of making changes based on assumptions.

Whether you're working on a small website or a complex web application, becoming familiar with DevTools can make your development workflow more efficient and help you build websites that are easier to maintain.

Rather than treating debugging as something that happens only after development, make it a regular part of your workflow. This allows you to identify problems earlier, verify your solutions, and deliver more reliable web experiences.

#10167. Like clockwork, the Pixel 11 is a way better buy after a few months [Video]

Like clockwork, the first quarter of the Pixel 11 brings discounts, and this shines a light on the base model that needs to be explored.

By this point, you don’t need me to tell you that this is a familiar design. It’s turning into a comfortable pair of slippers at this point in time. Nothing is wrong, and nothing particularly stands out. A safe but samey design that is inoffensive.

I hoped that the maximum brightness bug would have been resolved by now with the screen. It creates a shadow around the edges of the display after a minute or so when in direct sunlight. Somewhat like blooming on a cheap LCD screen. It does diminish what is otherwise a great display.

The thickness has started to feel like a relic of the 2023 design that feels a little dated now. I have been using the Pixel 11 without a case most of the time, which mitigates it, but throw a case on and it’s a chunky boi. The added thickness would probably have been acceptable had the extra space been filled with more battery, but it feels like that boat has set sail a long time ago. A slimmer profile probably would have helped differentiate without deviating too much from last year’s phone.

After months of using the Pixel 11, the move of the NFC module to the top edge has been huge for me because my car uses an NFC terminal to lock and unlock when you’re without a set of car keys. With the Pixel 10, it worked but not quite as smoothly. It’s one of those inconsequential changes that makes a real-world difference.

Gemini Intelligence has been something I’ve started to rely on quite heavily. I love that I get a prompt to add an appointment to my calendar, and all of the important details have been taken care of, like the location, extra contact details, the works.

I’m not sure what the hold-up is for adding wider functionality. It’s actively hurting things, in my opinion. Google Messages is great, but some of the automation functions locked in there mean I haven’t been saving any time with Gemini’s prowess at doing the legwork of keeping my life up to date.

I’ve tried using Pause Point, but found it’s just easier to leave my phone face down rather than use an app to try and stop using my phone…maybe if we all did that, so many of the world’s ills wouldn’t be so pronounced. Just a thought.

The November Pixel Drop has also made some notable changes to the Pixel 11 experience that I can’t believe were held back since launch. If you waited and bought in the last few weeks with the substantial discounts kicking around, you objectively get a better phone today than at launch.

The improvements don’t overhaul things, just enhance or heighten the fluidity and experience above what was available in the months post-launch. More cohesive theming, little tweaks to the UI; it’s not reinventing the Pixel 11, just augmenting it in ways that would have been nice back in August.

I don’t really have much to complain about with the Pixel 11 lifespan even after months of charging. Yes, charging is still slow, but you only need to charge once per day. If we were talking about a phone with bad battery life and slow charging, I’d have more to complain about. However, it’s still solid and dependable.

The camera continues to impress without really improving drastically. I can’t help but feel that every couple of generations, the Pro camera setup should be passed on to the base Pixel, then we get a newer, improved system on that specific Pro model. It’d allow for quicker improvements and maybe, dare I say it, make the camera system cheaper as parts get reused. If anything, it would help Google’s supposed environmental pledges by preventing camera components from hitting landfill.

A lot of people will tell you the best camera is the one you have with you, and this hasn’t ever been a problem for a recent Pixel. I can count on one hand the frustrations I have felt with the Pixel 11 camera since it landed on my desk on launch day. I am happier than a year ago with the base Pixel 10 than the 11, which is to say this is a bigger leap or a return to form.

Having the ability to tune Camera Looks was something I have loved, but you might just fall into using one tuned look that fits your own personal style. There is an element of this that might change the perception of the Pixel 11 camera consistency versus its predecessors. What I mean is that while it does still excel at point-and-shoot, the fact that Looks are baked into the JPEG could be a problem. There are times when I’ve thought that an image might have been better using default settings.

That said, I have stuck to “Shadows” with Contrast set to -5, Highlights to -5, Shadows to -5.5, warmth to 0.5, and Grain to “Strong.” That is a recipe I’ll probably stick with for a long time to come. It’s still weird that we can do all this at the camera level with a Pixel. Whether “normies” even use the options or just stick to the out-of-the-box default settings is another discussion for another day.

At $699, the Pixel 11 becomes a decent buy for a lot of people. For others, the distinct lack of power is something that can’t ever be overcome. I would buy this for a relative because it’ll do everything they could ever want and more. For a friend who cares about having the cutting edge in their pocket? Probably not.

A lot of the problems over the past few years on Pixel phones have come from the price tag.

Even with the discounts in and around Black Friday, the fact that you can get a device like the Galaxy S25 Ultra that’ll have better specs and lots of valuable features for around the same price. The last few generations of the Pixel have been harder to recommend because of the drawbacks versus the cost and the competition.

I said a few months ago that there is a good phone under the price hikes, and that remains unchanged. However, there are better products from the year prior that won’t compromise and are even cheaper. In a silo, you’re getting a 7/10 product at a 3/10 price. Just how that changes depends on forthcoming updates. One saving grace is that no Pixel is ever quite the same months after it rolls off the production line. A few Pixel Drops, even more discounts, and maybe a true seal of approval might be easier.

To put it into context, this was never a $900 phone and arguably never should have been. That’s somewhat a failing on Google’s part in more ways than one. At least it’s down to a palatable price that should be the Pixel baseline.

#10163. How Agent Anomaly Detection scores on benchmarks we didn't write

AI agents now read email, book travel and move money for the people who use them. Every tool call also pulls in text that someone else wrote, and whoever controls that text can try to steer the agent. OWASP calls this agent goal hijack and ranks it first in its Top 10 for Agentic Applications (2026).

In September we announced Agent Anomaly Detection in Private Preview on Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform. It reads your agents' traces and logs asynchronously, without adding latency, and flags sessions where an agent acts outside its intended boundaries. We tested this feature against benchmarks for this top risk of agent goal hijack.

Security tools tend to look good on tests their own team wrote, so we ran five non-Google-written, public agent-security benchmarks through Agent Anomaly Detection without changing anything. In four of them, the harmful instruction hides in data that a tool returns. In the fifth, the user asks for harm directly.

The model itself didn't follow about half of the harmful instructions it read. It refused three in four direct requests for harm, and ignored or failed at about 30% of the instructions hidden in tool output. Of the ones that were executed, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% for post-execution review. It raised false alarms on 2% to 16% of benign sessions, depending on the tools the agent uses. Poisoned memory is the clear gap: when instructions planted in the agent’s long-term memory made it act, Agent Anomaly Detection caught 4 of 18.

We ran each benchmark case through an agent built with the Agent Development Kit (ADK) using gemini-2.5-flash, on the benchmark's simulated apps. The agent sent its traces and logs to Agent Anomaly Detection, as a customer's agent would. Then the benchmark's own Python check read the final state of the apps, such as whether money left the account, and labeled the session. Deterministic code decides the label, not a model, so an injected instruction can't mislead the grader, and Agent Anomaly Detection cannot cheat the benchmark. Each agent first ran at least 300 benign sessions, because Agent Anomaly Detection learns how each agent usually behaves.

We also recorded whether a tool returned the harmful instruction to the agent. In many cases, the agent never opens the email or file that holds it. So each harmful session ends one of three ways:

In AgentHarm, the user sends the request, so the agent always reads it.

The five benchmarks are AgentDojo (NeurIPS 2024), InjecAgent (ACL 2024 Findings), Agent Security Bench (ASB, ICLR 2025), AgentDyn (2026) and AgentHarm (ICLR 2025). ASB plants the instruction in the agent's long-term memory. In all, Agent Anomaly Detection scored 1,774 sessions.

Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% of the harmful instructions that the agent carried out, and raised a few false alarms on ordinary office work. It's strongest on goal hijack and tool misuse, and weakest on poisoned memory.

Each finding carries a threat category, and four of Agent Anomaly Detection’s five categories align with the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications. We graded each OWASP category on what this study showed, not on what Agent Anomaly Detection is designed to catch.

Public benchmarks concentrate on goal hijack and tool misuse, so our evidence is strongest there. We did not find public benchmarks with labeled agent traces for cascading failures or for identity and privilege abuse. Shared benchmarks for those categories would help the whole field.

By benchmark, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 85% of the carried-out harmful instructions on AgentDojo, 87% on InjecAgent, 99% on AgentDyn and 81% on AgentHarm. Over all harmful sessions, including the ones that the agent never read or resisted, it flagged 45% to 62%. In the 141 sessions where the agent never read the instruction, nothing happened, and it flagged just 1.

Results held across the apps. In 8 of the 11 apps, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged at least 95% of the carried-out harmful instructions. The lowest rate was in AgentDojo's Slack app, at 63%. Many of those instructions ask for one small action, such as a page visit, which looks like normal browsing.

Agent Anomaly Detection also flags many requests that the agent refuses. In AgentHarm, the agent refused most requests and carried out 108 of 412, and Agent Anomaly Detection still flagged 55% of the refused sessions, because the request sits in the user's own message, which it reads. When the agent didn't follow an instruction hidden in tool output, it flagged only 11% to 25%, because the instruction sits deep inside a tool result.

False alarms were rare on ordinary office work: on the three injection benchmarks, whose benign sessions are office tasks, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 2% to 4% of benign sessions. On AgentHarm it flagged 16%. Those benign tasks are twins of the harmful ones, so they post to social media, run terminal commands and download files. High-impact tools in normal use look more like misuse.

Agent Anomaly Detection did less well in five areas. It flagged only 11% to 25% of the instructions that the agent resisted, because a resisted instruction changes little in how the agent behaves. It missed more small actions, like single page visits, because they look like normal work. And it raised more false alarms for agents whose everyday work uses high-impact tools. If your agent is one of them, expect more findings at first, and triage them by severity and threat category.

The other two areas are new agents and poisoned memory. Some Agent Anomaly Detection methods need a few hundred sessions of history: on AgentDyn, it flagged 33% of carried-out harmful instructions when each agent had about 220 benign sessions, and 99% with 360. And it caught only 4 of the 18 sessions where poisoned memory made the agent act.

Protect your agents with defense in depth. Use controls that stop harmful actions before they run, and Agent Anomaly Detection to find what gets through.

We used one agent model and simulated apps, and the benchmarks' benign tasks are likely simpler than your production traffic, so your false-alarm rate can differ. Agent Anomaly Detection returned no result for about 6% of the sessions we sent; counting those as misses lowers the overall rates by 1 to 2 points.

Agent Anomaly Detection is in Private Preview. To request access, read the Agent Anomaly Detection overview.

From Files to Cited Answers in One API Call: Managed Ingestion and SearchDocuments in Agent Retrieval

Accelerating development for massive Git repositories

#10160. How to Add Idempotency Keys to a Node.js API with PostgreSQL

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A mobile app sends POST /orders. The server creates the order, but the response is lost when the phone switches from Wi-Fi to 4G. The app sees a timeout and retries. Now the customer has two orders, and your support team has a refund to process.

Retrying is the right thing for a client to do. The problem is that POST is not idempotent: RFC 9110 defines GET, PUT and DELETE as idempotent methods, but not POST or PATCH. Repeating a POST can repeat its side effects.

An idempotency key fixes this. The client generates a unique key for each logical operation and sends it with every attempt. The server does the work once, stores the response against the key, and returns the stored response for any retry. The IETF HTTPAPI working group is standardizing this pattern as the Idempotency-Key HTTP header field, and this tutorial follows its recommendations.

We'll build it with Node.js, Express 5 and PostgreSQL. By the end you'll have a table that stores keys, a function that safely claims a key under concurrent requests, an Express route that replays stored responses, and a client that retries without creating duplicates.

The client's job is simple: generate a key, usually a UUID, and send the same key on every retry of the same operation. The draft defines the header value as a quoted string:

The server's job is harder, because every request falls into one of five cases:

To tell case 3 from case 4, the server stores a fingerprint of the request alongside the key. A matching fingerprint means a genuine retry; a different one means key reuse. Case 5 is the one most implementations get wrong, because it only shows up under concurrency. A database constraint handles it for us.

Create a project and install the two dependencies:

The key table records who sent the key, the request fingerprint, whether the work has finished, and the response to replay:

Two details matter here. The primary key includes client_id, so keys are scoped per client: two customers who happen to generate the same value can't see each other's responses. And locked_until lets the server recover a key whose request crashed halfway, which we'll use in Step 3.

The draft defines the header value as a structured field string, which means it arrives wrapped in double quotes. Many clients send it without quotes, so the parser below accepts both and limits the characters and length it will store:

The fingerprint has to be identical for identical requests, even if a client serializes the JSON body with its properties in a different order. Sorting object keys before hashing takes care of that. The hash uses SHA-256 from Node's built-in crypto module:

Including the method and path means a key reused on a different endpoint counts as a mismatch, not as a retry.

The tempting approach is to SELECT the key, and insert it if nothing comes back. Under concurrency that fails: two retries arriving a few milliseconds apart both see no row, and both do the work.

Instead, let the primary key decide. INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING either inserts the row or does nothing, atomically, even across many application processes. The PostgreSQL INSERT documentation describes this behavior in detail. If the insert returns a row, this request owns the key. If not, we read the existing row and decide which case we're in:

The UPDATE in the last step is also atomic. If two retries find the same expired lock, only one of them matches the locked_until < now() condition and gets the key. If the row disappears between the two queries, for example because it was released after a failure, the function reports in_progress and the client simply retries.

Set LOCK_SECONDS comfortably above the slowest time your operation takes. If the lock expires while the original request is still running, a retry can take over and run the work a second time.

Once a request owns the key, it creates the order and marks the key as completed. Both writes happen in one transaction, so there is no moment where the order exists but the key still says in_progress, or the other way round. Note that a transaction in node-postgres has to use a single client checked out from the pool, as the node-postgres transaction documentation explains:

releaseKey runs when the work fails. Deleting the claim lets the client retry with the same key instead of waiting for the lock to expire. It only deletes rows that are still in progress, so a completed response is never thrown away.

The route validates the body first. A request with invalid input never touches the key table, so the client can fix the input and resend with the same key. Error responses use the application/problem+json format from RFC 9457, which the idempotency draft also uses in its examples:

The server side only works if the client keeps the key stable. The most common client bug is generating a new key inside the retry loop, which turns every retry into a new operation. Generate the key once per logical action, before the first attempt:

The catch branch is the reason idempotency keys exist. After a timeout, the client can't know whether the order was created. With a stable key, retrying is safe either way: if the first attempt succeeded, the retry returns the stored order; if it didn't, the retry creates it. The random jitter spreads retries out, so many clients recovering from the same outage don't all hit the server at once.

In a browser or mobile app, store the key with the pending action, for example in the form state or a local queue. If the user taps "Place order" again after an error, reuse it. Only generate a new key when the user starts a genuinely new action.

Keys can't live forever, or the table grows without limit. The draft leaves the retention period to the server but says it should be published in the API documentation, so clients know how long a retry stays safe. Twenty-four hours covers most retry scenarios. A scheduled job can delete older rows in batches:

Run it every few minutes from cron or your job scheduler until it deletes zero rows. Deleting in batches keeps each transaction short, and the created_at index from Step 1 keeps the inner query fast. A retry that arrives after its key has expired is treated as a new request, which is why the retention period belongs in your documentation.

Start the server with node server.js, then send the same request twice:

The second request lists the properties in a different order, and it's still recognized as a retry because of the canonical fingerprint. Both responses carry the same order ID, and the orders table contains one row.

Now change the quantity while keeping the key. The server answers 422, because the key now belongs to a different request:

To see the concurrent case, add await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, 3000)); before completeKey in the route, restart the server, and fire two requests with a new key at the same time:

One request prints 201 and the other 409. Without the ON CONFLICT claim, both would have printed 201 and created two orders. Remove the delay when you're done.

This design guarantees that the database work in Step 4 happens once per key. Two situations need extra care:

Idempotency keys don't replace other safeguards either. A unique constraint on a natural business key, such as one payment per invoice, still catches duplicates that arrive with different keys, for example when a user submits the same form from two tabs.

With one table, an atomic claim and a transaction that stores the response next to the work, POST endpoints become safe to retry. Clients get a simple rule: generate one key per action, reuse it on every retry. The server takes care of the rest, including the concurrent retries that a SELECT-then-INSERT approach misses.

The same pattern works for PATCH endpoints and for webhooks you send to other systems. Start with the endpoints where a duplicate costs real money or confuses a customer, and publish your key format and retention period in the API documentation so client developers know what to send.

#10158. Caveman repurposes old mining rig for local AI, pools 72GB VRAM across mismatched GPUs

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

A fine display of redneck engineering and research.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Given that GPUs with any sizable amount of VRAM or horsepower are now made of unobtainium, enthusiasts looking to run LLMs locally try all sorts of tricks to use larger models. True to their moniker, X user Caveman Tech combined wildly different cards in the same rig, but not in the usual manner. They brought back a forgotten relic: a mining rig with PCIe x1 slots. Much to their surprise, the setup worked far better than it had any right to.

For their first trick, Tech used a Quadro RTX 4000 all on its own on the PCIe x1 slot, as opposed to the standard x16 connector. Running Qwen3-8B yielded the same 56 tokens/s output speed as before, with the only drawback being a longer model load at eight seconds — but unless you're running several models, you'll want to keep it in VRAM anyway. A number of software and hardware tweaks yielded even better results, upping tok/s to 96.

🚨 We just discovered that a PCIe x1 mining riser does NOT slow down local AI. I took a Quadro RTX 4000, jammed it in an x1 slot on my mining rig, and tested it like crazy. The result breaks what everyone assumes about x1 risers. 🤯 ⚡ Qwen3 8B: 56 tokens/sec. The same speed as a full x16 slot. ⚡ While it's writing, the x1 link carries just a few KB per second. It's basically asleep. ⚡ The only cost: 8 seconds to load the model. That's it. Then we went hunting for hidden boosts 🔍 🔥 +24% speed in long chats by switching one memory setting (f16 KV cache) 🔥 1.75x total output: 4 requests at once = 96 tok/s 🔥 +7% tokens per watt by capping it at 110 W 🔥 A 65,000-token conversation squeezed into 8 GB And the twist: NVIDIA locks memory overclocking on this Quadro. The driver allows exactly 0 MHz. 🔒 Every result answer-checked, every number measured. That old mining rig full of x1 risers? It's an AI rig now. 🔥November 25, 2026

This result alone is contrary to the general zeitgeist that says low-bandwidth slots are not useful for AI work. Much like many comments, you're probably thinking that while the setup is serviceable, the true test comes when using multiple GPUs at once that need to talk to each other, so Caveman Tech obliged.

The second setup involved three cards in what can be described as a staggeringly asymmetric configuration: a CMP 170HX with 64 GB of VRAM on the primary PCIe x16 slot, the aforementioned Quadro 4000 (8 GB) on x1. The chosen model was now GPT-OSS-120B, and the pooled VRAM totaled 72 GB.

As a slap back to the naysayers, this configuration held up surprisingly well, as it turns out that with layer splitting (wherein the harness splits the model's layers across several GPUs), even the PCIe x1 link was only 10% busy. In this setup, cards only transmit the calculation results between them, not model weights.

The overall token generation speed dropped from 121 tok/s to 91 tok/s, likely due to the raw performance difference between the 170HX and the other two cards. As a point of comparison, letting the model spill to system RAM yielded only 52 token/s, less than half the original speed.

You said x1 breaks once cards have to talk to each other. Fair. So I tested it. 🧪 Rig: CMP 170HX 64GB, Quadro RTX 4000 on x1, GTX 1660 on x4. No P2P, so every byte between cards goes through the CPU. 📊 gpt-oss-120b: • 1 card (170HX): 121 tok/s • Split onto the x1 card (layer split): 92 tok/s. The x1 link sat under 10% busy • Same split, tensor mode (cards talk every layer): 101 tok/s writing, but prompt reading drops to 671 (from 1,113) with x1 ~45% busy • Spill to CPU RAM instead: 52 tok/s So you're half right 👇 ✅ Generation: x1 holds up even when cards talk every layer ❌ Prompt processing in tensor split: that's where x1 hurts ✅ A second card on x1 still beats spilling to RAM by 1.8x Next: a device-order trick I think wins most of that gap back. Testing it. 👀November 25, 2026

Caveman Tech did try an aggressive configuration in tensor mode, wherein all cards handle a portion of all layers, and only weights and the KV cache are split between them. That did bump the output to 101 tok/s, but prompt reading fell from the original 1,113 tok/s to 671, with the PCIe link 45% busy.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The conclusions are useful and straightforward: with judicious configuration, adding a card on a x1 link on a cheap mining rig is a perfectly reasonable way to expand the available VRAM, letting one use larger, smarter models that would be otherwise slow or downright impractical to run if they had to resort to system memory. It'd be interesting to see experiments with more than one GPU on x1 slots, as in theory the same conclusion should still hold.

#10153. Catch Go bugs that fuzzing misses with Gemini and a constraint solver

I help lead Google's agent skills program, currently with 175 agent skills that supplement community collections like go-skills. To keep that many skill files consistent, I wrote skill-lint, a standalone Go CLI that checks SKILL.md files and applies LLM-generated line edits before they ship. Even with skill-lint's own unit and fuzz test suite, a small slice or line-number mistake inside its Markdown parsers or edit applier can sit dormant until an unusual file or edit range triggers a runtime panic in the linter.

In Go, every slice or index operation that can panic at runtime compiles down to an explicit bounds-check trap in machine code, after the compiler's optimizer strips out the checks it can already rule out. To hunt for crashes, I compiled key functions from my code to see which panic traps survived optimization, and Gemini 4 Argon translated the path to each trap into rules for the Z3 constraint solver. An LLM-written solver model isn't a sound proof system, but it gives the agent an independent way to reason about control flow and synthesize concrete Go reproducers, and every crash it finds is real because go test runs the offending bytes directly. That workflow caught all three bugs I planted in test parsers and turned up three latent bugs in skill-lint.

In this post, I'll walk through how the Gemini and Z3 pipeline works, why go test -fuzz missed two of those crashes (and the one-line change that fixes it), and where this approach fits in a day-to-day Go workflow.

A recent post from Anthropic showed how formal verification catches latent bugs in C and C++ by having frontier AI models write machine-checked Rocq proofs across OSS-Fuzz libraries. Instead of sampling random inputs like a fuzzer, a formal tool works backward from each failure condition to solve for the exact input that triggers it. Full theorem proving is still too heavy for most day-to-day Go projects, so I wanted to see how much of that bug-hunting power an agent can get from an LLM and a constraint solver in about a minute per function.

In C and C++, reading or writing past the end of a buffer corrupts memory. Go catches out-of-bounds access at runtime instead, compiling every slice or index operation it can't prove safe into an explicit branch to runtime.panicBounds that panics, crashing the process unless recovered.

Before generating machine code, Go's SSA prove pass already eliminates most of those checks by tracking slice lengths and loop variables. That optimization pass leaves behind only the runtime.panicBounds traps the compiler couldn't rule out on its own, giving the solver a short list of candidate crash sites to target.

I compiled each target with GOOS=linux GOARCH=riscv64 go build -gcflags="-S -d=ssa/check_bce/debug=1" (-S prints the assembly listing and ssa/check_bce/debug=1 logs every surviving bounds check) and passed both the Go source and the disassembly to the model. Looking at Go source alone, you can't see which bounds checks the compiler already removed or where a register gets truncated to 8 bits; in the disassembly, both show up. I targeted RISC-V because it's the instruction set Anthropic's post targets for its Rocq proofs, and Go cross-compiles to it out of the box.

From there, Argon read the Go source and disassembly and returned a Z3 script modeling the path to each surviving trap, along with a patched function whenever it flagged a bug. Z3 then checked whether those constraints could be satisfied, and whenever it found a crashing input, go test replayed those bytes against the real function. Using an LLM saved me from translating assembly into solver constraints by hand. Argon's translation dominated the runtime, at about a minute per function, while Z3 itself finished in milliseconds.

Anthropic's approach relies on interactive theorem proving, where experts model machine semantics and Rocq checks LLM-generated proofs against those rules. In contrast, my workflow uses a constraint solver based on satisfiability modulo theories (SMT), relying on Argon to translate assembly directly into Z3 constraints. While a constraint solver doesn't provide a complete correctness proof without a semantic model, it is highly effective for finding bugs. Z3's check-sat command returns sat when input constraints can be satisfied and unsat when they cannot.

A sat answer ("definitely buggy") comes with concrete bytes aimed at the trap, and go test runs them against the real function. Once go test panics, you have a concrete bug reproducer regardless of whether Argon's model was sound. Like a Bloom filter for bugs, an unsat answer only means "maybe correct." Trading away a correctness proof is what makes the approach so practical: you get most of the bug-finding value of formal methods for a tiny fraction of the effort.

Anthropic's setup proved no-crash properties on whole binaries under a 64 MB budget; I checked individual functions on fresh 64-byte slices where cap(buf) == len(buf), so any slice past len(buf) panics. (If you want the full-binary Rocq setup for Go, theorem-labs/oss-verify includes a Go 1.27 runtime overlay that makes the runtime single-threaded and deterministic.)

For the fuzzing baseline, I ran unseeded go test -fuzz on Go 1.27.3 against each planted bug: one 3-second run and three 60-second runs, each with a fresh fuzz cache so a short time budget wouldn't explain a miss.

I started with three safe parsers to make sure the pipeline didn't flag clean code. The compiler's prove pass removed every bounds check in a TOML key parser on its own, and Z3 returned unsat on the remaining traps in an HTTP header scanner and a varint decoder.

Then I wrote three parsers with subtle panics. Here's the first one, ParseTwoChunkFrame:

In the RISC-V disassembly, the bug is much easier to see:

total := hdrLen + bodyLen adds two uint8 values, so MOVBU wraps the sum at 256 before the length guard checks it, while endBody adds the full 64-bit ints. Argon turned those instructions into a Z3 query asking whether a fresh slice of up to 64 bytes can pass the guard and still fail the endBody check at 0x0092:

Z3 returned a 59-byte counterexample with hdrLen = 48 and bodyLen = 236: their uint8 sum wraps from 284 down to 28 (so 28 + 4 ≤ 59 passes the guard), and buf[endHdr:endBody] then slices to index 288 and panics.

At 59 bytes, 19.4% of all possible (hdrLen, bodyLen) pairs trigger this crash. Yet a 3-second fuzz run missed it, and so did three 60-second runs.

Go checks a slice expression like buf[endHdr:endBody] against the slice's capacity, not its length, and Go's fuzzer rarely passes a slice without spare capacity. In a 10-second probe of 386,741 fuzz calls, 89% of inputs were views into a shared ~100 MB buffer, and only 0.07% had cap == len. With that much spare capacity, endBody (at most 514) never exceeds cap(buf), so the buggy parser returns bytes past the end of buf instead of panicking.

The second target, ExtractQuotedToken, hit the same blind spot when a trailing backslash pushed the index one byte past len(buf). Z3 caught it immediately with the 2-byte input []byte{'"', '\\'}, while the fuzzer absorbed the overrun in spare capacity.

Clipping capacity to length with a full slice expression turns any slice past len into a panic:

After that change, the fuzzer crashed both ParseTwoChunkFrame and ExtractQuotedToken in all three runs (median 0.28 s and 0.17 s). The third target, ParseMagicPacket, was a control because its signed int8 length bug panics on a negative index regardless of capacity, and the fuzzer found it in every run either way. Fuzzing still can't give you an unsat signal across the remaining paths, which is how I checked the three safe parsers and Argon's patches.

Next, I pointed the pipeline at skill-lint itself. A whole Go CLI is too large to hand to a solver directly. Even a 10-line parser links 1,559 symbols from the Go runtime, and the 28 MB skill-lint binary has 32,321 symbols in total. Following Functional Core, Imperative Shell, I left CLI flags, network calls, and file I/O to go test -fuzz and go test -race, and ran the solver only on pure functions and slice helpers extracted from file-reading code.

Of the five functions I tested, two scanners came back unsat (including a CRLF line splitter with six surviving bounds checks), and the other three had bugs. The sharpest was in ApplyReplacementChunks, which validates LLM-generated {StartLine, EndLine} edits against the initial line count and sorts them bottom-to-top so earlier edits don't shift later line numbers:

Z3 immediately returned sat because sorting by StartLine doesn't prevent two chunks from overlapping. In the extracted helper, which checks bounds once up front, replacing lines 3 and 4 of a 4-line file with one line shrinks lines to 3 elements, so a second chunk spanning lines 2 through 4 evaluates lines[4:] and panics with slice bounds out of range [4:3]. In the shipped ApplyChunks, a per-iteration bounds check turns that case into a misleading range error, while overlapping chunks that stay within len(lines) silently overwrite the earlier edit. Rejecting overlapping chunks after the sort fixes both.

The other two bugs were similar accounting errors. In the local fixer, target line numbers were checked once per violation before the inner chunk loop applied deletions that shrank the slice (a latent panic if a fixer ever returns a second chunk after a deletion, though DeleteLineFix only returns one today). In the prompt packer, a truncation check reserved a flat 100 bytes for a ~70-byte wrapper plus file path, overshooting the 2 MB context budget in the shipped code when a path exceeds about 30 bytes and panicking in the extracted helper.

When you check a function modularly like this, the target has to include its own entry-point guards (like the bounds check and sort in ApplyReplacementChunks). If an internal helper assumes preconditions that are only enforced by its caller or stated in a doc comment, Z3 will happily invent inputs that violate them. Because each extracted helper included its entry guards, false positives weren't an issue: across all six buggy functions in my tests, the first sat assignment Z3 returned triggered a real panic in go test without needing to filter out non-crashing candidates or loop back into the solver.

Once I pulled these helpers away from file I/O, a 3-second fuzz run caught all three bugs too. To make sure Argon wasn't leaning on source comments in my test files, I also reran the skill-lint targets with all comments stripped; Argon and Z3 still caught all three bugs and returned unsat on the two scanners and all three patches.

Staying entirely inside the target language makes a whole class of bugs hard for an agent to see. When an LLM reviews Go source directly, a line like if int(total)+4 > len(buf) looks like a textbook bounds check, and when it runs go test -fuzz, target-language runtime details like spare slice capacity or multi-step precondition checks can hide the crash indefinitely. Asking the agent to translate Go and assembly into a constraint solver forces a change of representation that strips away surface syntax and makes every bit width and slice bound explicit, while go test turns any solver hit back into a concrete Go reproducer.

For most Go code, fuzzing pure helpers with slice capacity clipped to length will catch bounds bugs in seconds. A solver is worth the extra minute on the traps that survive both the compiler's range prover and a quick fuzz run, especially in parsers and editors that compute offsets from multi-field headers or line ranges.

Single-threaded solver models don't see data races or deadlocks, so concurrent code still belongs with the race detector, deterministic concurrency testing, and Go 1.27's goroutine leak profiles.

If you're experimenting with solvers or coding agents on Go, let's compare notes on X or LinkedIn.

From Files to Cited Answers in One API Call: Managed Ingestion and SearchDocuments in Agent Retrieval

Did the user ask for this? Securing AI agents against intent hijacking with Semantic governance policies

#10149. One-Shot Any Web App with Gradio's gr.HTML

#10147. How SD Card Photo and Video Recovery Works: A Practical Guide With a JPEG Carving Example

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

For photographers, videographers and content creators, an SD card is more than a storage device. It holds the raw material behind a finished project: wedding photographs, travel footage, product demos and drone recordings that may be impossible to recreate. Data loss can happen at almost any stage of that workflow, from a folder deleted to make space to a card formatted before the footage was backed up.

This article explains why files on a card become unavailable, what to do in the first minutes, how recovery tools find data, and how to compare options. It includes a small Python example that recovers JPEG files from a disk image, so you can see the mechanism for yourself. It also compares free open-source tools with paid graphical ones, using criteria you can apply yourself. Recovery is never guaranteed, and tool features change, so confirm details against each vendor’s current documentation.

Recovery is not just a matter of finding a deleted filename. Several different problems can make files unavailable, and they call for different approaches.

These steps cannot guarantee recovery, but they reduce avoidable risk.

Most tools combine two methods, and understanding them helps you read scan results.

Carving has a cost. Because file system information is ignored, names and folder structure are usually lost, which is why scan results often show generic names. Fragmented files can also be recovered incomplete, since the tool assumes the pieces are stored one after another. Free tools build on these same ideas. Wondershare Recoverit V15, which also offers a free version for Windows and Mac, adds a graphical interface, while PhotoRec and ddrescue stay closer to the command line.

The script below is a teaching sketch, not a recovery product. It reads a raw disk image, finds JPEG start markers, and writes out the bytes up to the last end marker before the next start. That detail matters because a JPEG can contain an embedded thumbnail with its own markers, so stopping at the first end marker would cut a file short.

A short test builds two real JPEGs, hides them in random bytes, and checks that both are carved and open as valid images:

Use it on an image rather than the card itself. On Linux you can make one with a read-only copy, for example with the GNU ddrescue tool, which is designed to copy data from failing media. Then run the carver against the image and save the output to a different drive. Real tools handle many more formats, trim files using header information, and deal with video containers, so treat this as a way to understand the idea.

The right choice depends on the problem, your platform and how comfortable you are with the command line. The table summarizes what each project’s own documentation states.

The order of steps matters more than the choice of tool. A cautious workflow on Linux or macOS looks like this:

Graphical tools follow the same logic and mainly change step 3, with thumbnails, filters and search that make large result sets easier to browse. Whether that is worth paying for depends on how many files you need to sift through and how comfortable you are on the command line. When comparing any tool, ask:

Features such as search by capture time, folder or AI-based tagging depend on metadata surviving the loss, and they narrow results without proving a file is intact. Treat them as convenience, not verification.

Recovering a video file does not necessarily mean recovering a playable video. A recording interrupted during writing can lack the structural information a player needs, and other files may be damaged in ways that block playback.

The distinction matters. Recovery retrieves missing or inaccessible data, while repair tries to fix damage that affects a file’s usability. Some cases need one, the other or both. Repair has limits too: if essential video data was overwritten or never recorded, software cannot recreate the original footage in full. After any recovery or repair, play the file all the way through and compare it with what you expect.

For a quick check of a recovered clip, ffprobe can show whether the container is readable:

An error or a missing duration suggests the file needs repair or is incomplete.

Often yes, if nothing has overwritten them. Stop using the card immediately and scan it or an image of it.

Not before recovery. Formatting changes file system structures and may make recovery harder.

Recovery retrieves missing data. Repair fixes a file that exists but will not open or play correctly.

Not always. PhotoRec and TestDisk are free and open source, while paid tools add a graphical interface and extras such as previews, filtering and video repair.

On a different drive from the card. Saving to the same card can overwrite data you still want.

SD card data loss creates two related challenges: getting files back, and working out which recovered files are useful. Stopping use of the card, working on an image and understanding the difference between file system recovery, carving and repair put you in a better position, whichever tool you choose. Graphical tools aim to make large result sets easier to navigate, and free tools such as PhotoRec and TestDisk remain solid options. Try your chosen tool on a copy first, verify what you recover, and keep a backup so you do not need to do this again.

#10145. What new Google Messages features are rolling out [November 2026]

Like other Google apps, Messages for Android A/B tests many features. It can take the RCS/SMS client a rather long time to actually launch new functionality in stable. From various reports, Google announcements, and devices we’ve checked, this is the current state of Messages.

These are Messages features that Google announced or have been spotted in the wild by beta users.

In the US, Google Messages now lets you “Text with Gemini” using the “Channels” feature introduced in November. This uses RCS Business Messaging and Gemini “tasks” (previously Spark) for a more proactive experience that also lets you direct the agent to accomplish actions from the Messages app. Use the chat with the four-color Gemini logo after setting up via gemini.google.com/channels.

Instead of pinned chats appearing at the top of the list, Google Messages is elevating them above all conversations as circular contact photos.

You now access “Search” — which has a redesigned page — from a centered floating action button (FAB) alongside “Start chat.”

Google has replaced the Gemini FAB with a button in the top-right corner next to your profile image.

Read receipts are now placed in a circle at the bottom-right corner of message bubbles and images. You swipe left to see timestamps and the encryption lock, while you can swipe right to reply. This first started appearing in August of 2024, but is now seeing wide beta and stable availability.

Enhanced Protection adds “Advanced detection to prevent spam and scams” across three features. Go to Google Messages settings > Protection & safety.

To see timestamps and the RCS encryption lock, you can now swipe left anywhere in a conversation. To reply to a message, you have to swipe right on the bubble. Tapping a message still shows the read receipts.

Instead of a top toolbar, long-pressing on a message or image opens a floating menu as the background blurs. Another press lets you copy just a part of the chat. Previously, you had to copy the entire message.

With iOS 27’s September launch, Android-iPhone conversations over RCS gain support for inline replies that include quotes, as well as photo reactions with the actual emoji instead of just the text description.

Jfc what is so hard about adding categories for conversations?

The new Google Keep integration lets you quickly create and share notes inside chats. If you don’t see a contextual suggestion to “Create list,” open the ‘plus’ menu for a new “Keep Notes” tile.

This will slide up a sheet that shows your list of notes and the “Create a note” button. Upon selecting or starting a note, you’ll see a send button in the bottom-right corner. Afterward, there’s a live preview in the conversation thread.

#10144. $120 wearable LED pin acts as a 'watermark for humans' to fight deepfakes

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The inventor describes this $120 device as 'a watermark for humans' designed to verify video footage is genuine.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Protector, a wearable ESP32-S3-powered pin packed with an LED matrix, is designed to be a "watermark for humans" who regularly appear in video recordings. Hardware researcher David Kramer says inspiration from the watermarking of currency led to this modern wearable, which pulses with an 'unpredictable' light sequence. "It flashes a light pattern every second that's generated from a secret key and the time, so nobody without the pin can fake it," explained the inventor in a communication with Tom's Hardware. Deepfake checkers can upload any suspicious video featuring a subject wearing a Protector pin to the iamnotfake.com website to verify whether the video is genuine.

Kramer has been working on The Protector for the past year as a solution to the growing deepfake and AI-fake problems in tech. He soon latched onto the idea of using color-pulsing LEDs as a video watermarking system. However, a solution involving implanted subdermal LEDs (in people's foreheads) was quickly shelved. Thankfully, a less painful, less permanent, and more investor-friendly wearable LED matrix pin was decided upon to commercialize this product.

Subjects should wear this deepfake-busting device conspicuously whenever they might be recorded on video, or whenever they want others to be able to verify their message independently. The Protector contains a secret private key and a precise clock. Its ESP32-S3 microcontroller takes these inputs and generates what is claimed to be a unique and unpredictable sequence of flashing color lights. If, for whatever reason, someone wants to check whether a video of a subject wearing The Protector has been deepfaked, they can upload it to a dedicated website for verification. It is explained that iamnotfake.com checks if the light patterns in the uploaded video match the ones generated by the device at the time of the recording. The Protector is not connected to the internet except briefly when charging, to keep its clock accurate.

Even if it works as intended, there seem to be some rather obvious ways a determined deepfaker could circumvent the safeguards built into The Protector. On the YouTube video page, there are numerous such suggestions. You could just edit the video to remove The Protector from the video, for example. Or someone could retain the video shell and just deepfake and/or lip-sync new content.

For some important context, though, this new device comes from the same inventor that created the clearly humorous physical NFT minting device we reported on in the summer. Kramer seems to be a staunch tech optimist, and there are signs that he might even be a satirist of the blockchain and LLM age. So it isn't easy to be sure whether The Protector is a genuinely pitched and productized anti-deepfake solution, or more of a comment on the escalating arms race between content creators and AI-generated impersonators. In conclusion, whether it is entirely deepfake-bulletproof or not, it is undeniably a techy, geeky, fun accessory.

Interested folks can pre-order The Protector for $120 today, with delivery promised to U.S. residents by December 18, "in time for Christmas." Buyers are limited to five units per order. You might, alternatively, apply to use the Synthetic Video Detector Nvidia NIM microservice, which was revealed at SIGGRAPH in July.

#10141. Review: The Alexa Tablet 12 Pro is an iPad alternative I actually want to use

I knew Amazon’s new flagship tablet was different when I found myself actually reaching for it.

Of course, you might be saying, that’s the job of a reviewer: to use the device throughout your daily life and let people know the good, the bad, and the ugly. But with the Alexa Tablet 12 Pro, I wasn’t just reaching for it when I thought a tablet might do the job. Over the past two weeks since receiving my review unit (the base nanomatte model), I’ve been pleasantly surprised by just how much I’ve preferred it over other devices. Hell, I’ve been reaching for Amazon’s slate over my Pixel 11 Pro XL, my MacBook Air, and even the pocket-friendly Lenovo Legion tablet I reviewed earlier this month, and considering this company’s history with tablets, I’m as shocked as anyone.

The original Kindle Fire launched back when I was in high school, and despite 15 years of product name changes, frequent model refreshes, and an attempt to slowly move up-market, even Amazon’s bottom-of-the-barrel pricing couldn’t make up for missing apps and terrible performance. But the Fire lineup is dead, replaced by a new lineup of more expensive “Alexa Tablets” that, nevertheless, remain a relative bargain in our ever-worsening gadget economy. This model, though, is a true iPad competitor. The Alexa Tablet 12 Pro attempts to correct every mistake Amazon has made with its tablet series over the past fifteen years, and somehow, for as low as $350 on sale, it basically pulls it all off.

Let’s get some of the most important changes out of the way immediately before we get to the real star of the show. Most of Amazon’s specs sheet here won’t impress you, but context really means everything. A Mediatek Dimensity 8400 chipset paired with (in my test model) 8GB of RAM and 128GB of UFS 3.1 storage sounds like any run-of-the-mill midrange Android tablet. That’s a massive leap ahead of anything powering previous Fire Tablet models, though, and the option to move to a 256GB model with UFS 4.0 and 16GB of RAM does exist if you really feel like you need some extra power. This is also a completely unsubsidized tablet; regardless of which model you pick, you won’t have to pay $20 to remove lockscreen ads, something that remains an issue on the cheaper Alexa Tablet 8 and 11 models.

Honestly, both the design and the overall feel of the hardware exceed my expectations; this is closer to Apple or Samsung’s premium tablet offerings than it is the Fire Tablets of yesteryear. It features an aluminum chassis with rounded edges and a thin, flat frame that — let’s be honest — looks right out of Apple’s typical iPad playbook. The power button doubles as a fingerprint sensor, while an indented groove below the volume buttons on the right side for its optional stylus. As someone who thinks this slate is better off for consumption than creation, it’s the one part of the design I question, though it also didn’t really bother me.

Without a doubt, the star of the show is this display. Amazon sent me the nanomatte version, available as a $50 upgrade over the $500 MSRP base model (the $650 256GB variant only comes in nanomatte). If you’re thinking of buying this tablet, the nanomatte model is without a doubt the one to spring for. It’s one of the nicer matte finishes I’ve seen on a device like this to date, and is absolutely the main reason I won’t stop reaching for this tablet. With a couple of tweaks — the saturation levels are a little too punchy out of the box for my liking, but Amazon’s “natural” color profile is a two-tap fix — this is as good as any non-OLED screen I can think of in recent memory. In fact, where Lenovo’s LCD panel had me wishing for next year’s rumored upgrade, I really never felt like OLED needed to be on Amazon’s flagship slate to make it worth my while.

No matter what I loaded up — The Odyssey, various episodes of TV or YouTube videos, the unofficial “Dusklight” port of Twilight Princess for Android — I just kept wanting to stare at this thing. I now totally understand why people spring for the nanomatte iPad Pro, and while I’m not trying to say this LCD holds a candle to one of the best OLED screens on the market right now, it’s nice to get this sort of finish on a tablet that doesn’t start at a whopping $1,800. (The iPad Pro itself starts at $1,200, but nanomatte is only available on 1TB or 2TB models, which I didn’t know until I sat down to write this review and is absolutely one of the dumbest things I’ve ever seen a company of Apple’s size do. I digress.)

Amazon’s quad-stereo speakers sound excellent for a device of this size. They get surprisingly loud without much distortion, perfect for YouTube videos or streaming random movies, or even casual music listening. I’d still opt for headphones or external speakers for something you really care about (once again, The Odyssey being the perfect example), but that’s not exactly rare for a tablet. The fingerprint sensor is fast and mostly accurate, and the flashless 13MP rear-facing camera is… here, should you desperately need one. A more useful 8MP front-facing camera runs along the right side of the device, and there’s a single USB-C port at the bottom of the slate. No expandable storage options, something I’m genuinely disappointed about considering this slate tops out at just 256GB.

Of course, we have to address the Amazon-sized elephant in the room: the software. Unlike the “Fire OS” fork of Android that has resulted in years of Christmas morning Google searches to find out how to get YouTube on the tablet you just unwrapped, the Alexa Tablet is just… running Android. That includes some obvious quality-of-life improvements, like being able to transfer apps and other data directly from your phone or an older tablet, but yes, it also means the Play Store is here, along with practically every Android app you could hope for. Gmail, Chrome, Docs, Photos — everything’s here, and the ability to no longer have to hope that a good-enough replacement exists on the Amazon Appstore is such a relief. Hell, Amazon’s own app marketplace isn’t even pre-installed.

Now, with all of that said, there are a couple of weird software quirks you should know going in. First, you’ll likely find a handful of applications unlisted from the Play Store as “unsupported” apps. This includes Amazon’s own software — the pre-loaded Amazon app is, bizarrely, just a web wrapper for the typical browser experience — and a smattering of random third-party applications, including the launcher Lawnchair, which I installed via the APK hosted on its website. That’s the second quirk: the default launcher is, as you might expect, themed around both Amazon and Alexa, complete with two unremovable widgets. It’s far from the worst home screen experience I’ve ever had, but a third-party launcher from the Play Store (or, again, from somewhere on the web) solves that problem. Save for the rare appearance of Amazon’s own dock and Alexa search bar when swiping back home, the third-party launcher experience here is great.

One more quick software positive: Amazon says it’ll support this tablet, along with the cheaper Alexa Tablet 11, through seven years of OS upgrades and security updates. We’ll have to see how frequent those updates arrive, but still — this experience feels built to last in a way Fire Tablets explicitly were not.

The Alexa Tablet 12 Pro’s specs might not sound particularly impressive on paper, but I’ve had absolutely no performance complaints. Granted, I haven’t exactly pushed the envelope here — that Zelda shout-out is the most I’ve demanded graphically — but web pages loaded fast without crashes or reloads, apps stayed alive in the background, and moving around the OS generally felt snappy. If this bar sounds low, you probably never used a legacy Fire Tablet. Battery life, meanwhile, has been excellent, with a single charge typically getting me through a few days of mixed use. Charging speeds could be better, though, with a full charge taking nearly two hours to power the slate back up. That’s not enough reason to skip out on Amazon’s tablet, obviously, but it is one of those advantages that Samsung’s (much) more expensive Galaxy Tab S-series holds over it.

It’s possible that my excitement over Amazon’s Alexa Tablet 12 Pro comes from a place of lowered expectations. I’m sure that’s true to some extent; again, 15 years of mostly-miserable experiences, up to and including the last time the company swung for a more premium slate, will do that to you. But considering Google’s disinterest in developing its own tablets and Samsung’s increasingly-ludicrous pricing for its higher-end options, I think this Alexa Tablet delivers a great alternative. Its specs are more than enough for the average Android user, and the things that I think people really care about on tablets — its display and its speakers — are among the best you can buy right now. Unless you really want to move into a four-figure price range for an OLED-equipped iPad or Galaxy Tab, I don’t really see a reason to shop around. And believe me, I’m still shocked.

That’s especially true during Black Friday, which — despite this tablet launching just weeks prior — is deeply discounted for the holiday season. You can grab the base model Alexa Tablet 12 Pro for just $350, but you should spring for the nanomatte model at $400. That’s $150 off a price I already think isn’t terrible for the money, and frankly, an even stronger recommend if and when you can score it on sale.

#10138. I’m so glad I didn’t give up on the Galaxy Z Fold 8

For a phone that heavily tested my patience in the first few weeks, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 has become an all-time favorite.

The amount of hype this phone brought with it at launch was unreal. Even then, I ultimately wound up going with the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra of the two models Samsung released earlier this year. It’s a form factor I was used to, but only looking back now do I realize just how much I was missing out on.

I still believe the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra is an excellent device. But the widely accepted blueprint for a consumer foldable might make a lateral move to what the Galaxy Z Fold 8 offers. That, I wouldn’t mind.

By comparison, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 is tiny. Its front display is squared-up and offers less vertical real estate than any other mainstream device coming out of Samsung. But that’s even more true compared to the lengthy profile the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra has. It’s called a “candy-bar foldable” for a reason.

The entire way I used my phone had to change, and the first few times I used the Galaxy Z Fold 8, I was left with a slightly bad taste in my mouth. I genuinely didn’t enjoy using it because of the amount of friction I was running into – not because the phone caused it, but because of my contradicting usage habits.

I always felt like I was missing out on something with the smaller front display, and I stubbornly tried to use the inner display in its wide orientation for most applications. I considered switching back, but I’m glad I stuck with it.

On the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra, or other standard foldables, I find myself using the inner display as the “main” display more often than not. But the Z Fold 8 gave me enough screen space in its closed state to come away with a good experience.

Since release, the majority of apps I use have been optimized in Android 17, or One UI 9. Social apps work well because that little bit of extra width leaves breathing room for videos to expand and fill the screen. Shopping and productivity apps are similar, and everything seems to fall into place.

I hit a wall with the inner display, too. It took some time for me to realize using the Fold 8 in landscape was genuinely the wrong choice 70% of the time. It sounds weird, but a phone that was designed to open up to a wider inner screen offers a better experience when you rotate it. At that point, you’re getting the best part of the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra, without the other cons.

Obviously, the wide inner display is great for videos and multitasking – not as good as the Ultra. But the inner display in its relative portrait orientation is excellent on hand for apps that need a little more room. It essentially boils down to whether you want a big screen for what you’re using the outer display for – a crazy thought, but not always the user experience.

Now, the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s outer display feels like the new normal to me, and using another phone feels foreign. The inner display has its place, and I automatically rotate it for nearly everything but media.

Even if this phone were just a proof of concept, it was successful. These smaller devices can work with some getting used to.

If given the choice between the design of the Galaxy Z Fold 8 and the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra once again, I’d now choose the smaller, wider Fold 8. Sure, it has a less capable camera loadout. The user experience does more to outweigh my ability to snap telephoto pictures, though.

This is a phone that has performed phenomenally in every major area, including how well it runs apps on Android and in battery life. I’m still getting more than a day’s worth of charge several months into using it. The same can’t be said for the Galaxy Z Fold 7.

It’s possible Samsung is working on a next-gen Galaxy Z Fold 9 with the build of the Fold 8 and the camera power of an “Ultra” model, but there’s nothing concrete. Until then, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 is riding the wave it created.
