#10096. iPhone Duo vs Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8: Here’s what each phone does better

I’ve been a loving user of the iPhone Duo since it launched just over a month ago. That said, it isn’t perfect. Over the same period of time, I’ve also had the opportunity to try out the Galaxy Z Fold 8, and while I’m certainly not a fan of OneUI, there’s a lot this phone does well that’s worth appreciating.

The absolute first thing you’ll notice about the Z Fold8 is that it’s lighter, by a decent margin. It comes in at 201 grams, vs the 254 grams of iPhone Duo and the 211 grams of iPhone 18 Pro. That means the Z Fold8 is just about as light as a normal sized smartphone, and this absolutely makes a difference.

The weight of iPhone Duo isn’t a deal-breaker, and I certainly appreciate having a titanium device again (vs aluminum on iPhone 17 Pro where I was consistently worried about denting it, especially with the aluminum extending to the camera bar) – but I’m sure the Z Fold8 being aluminum contributes a fair bit to how light it is.

The width of iPhone Duo feels just about perfect, but from the first day I’ve felt that it could be a little bit taller, just so you can fit slightly more content on your screen without your keyboard cutting it off. Z Fold8 is pretty much exactly what I’d like to see with a future iteration of iPhone Duo.

It’s still easy to reach the top of the screen without any hand gymnastics, and you would theoretically be able to see more content above the keyboard height.

That said, because of a difference in software design (OneUI using opaque navigation bars vs iOS keeping them transparent), you actually end up having about the same amount of readable content on both devices – even when you disable the additional keyboard toolbars and shrink the default font on OneUI.

First and foremost, I do appreciate the rounded corners on iPhone Duo. While it does cause for some extra padding and wasted space at times (and creates reachability challenges for left-handed users), it feels much nicer to hold a phone without a sharp corner digging into your hand. Granted, if you’re a leftie with an iPhone Duo, you’ll still feel the sharp corner on the outer screen.

While a much smaller point, iPhone Duo’s horizontal rear camera layout also makes a big difference. It means you can easily rest your finger underneath the camera bar, wheres on the Z Fold8 it feels hard to hold without the camera being in the way of your finger.

Lastly, I do appreciate Apple’s decision to go with an under display selfie camera on the inner display. Does this mean the inner camera takes rather bad photos in unideal conditions? Yes. That said, if you’re actually interested in taking a photo, the iPhone Duo offers two much better ways of taking selfies, so I don’t really take a major issue with this.

The fact that your actual content experience is uninterrupted is a big difference maker, and this isn’t something I thought I’d really care for at first, but it’s proven to be worthwhile. It does come with one compromise: iPhone Duo can’t offer Face Unlock on the inner screen because of the lower quality, but I’m hopeful this can be improved in later hardware iterations.

Another major point is creases. With iPhone Duo, Apple implemented a nano texture display layer to reduce crease visibility. Frankly though, after a month of usage, I’m not convinced this is actually necessary. On the Samsung, I never really see the crease while looking at the display head on, which is the same situation for iPhone Duo. On both devices, it’s only really visible off-axis.

Ultimately, we don’t know what an iPhone Duo without a nano texture would look like (unless you remove your nano texture layer, which you should not do) – so it’s possible that would’ve creased far worse than with the nano texture. That said, given how the Samsung crease looks with a glossy display, I think it’s possible that Apple can ship an iPhone Duo without a nano texture layer in the future.

All in all, these phones are both excellent displays of the engineering talents at both companies. Obviously, Samsung has been making folding phones for nearly 8 years now, while the iPhone Duo is Apple’s first foldable. Future iterations of iPhone Duo will likely get lighter, and maybe even a bit taller.

There likely isn’t much cross shopping going on between these two devices, so I won’t be making an outright verdict here. Ultimately, OS is a bigger factor than most of the hardware differences. If I had to sum it up though, I’d say my most optimal device would be a combination of the two: ideally iPhone Duo would be similarly thin and light to the Galaxy Z Fold8, while being a little bit taller and retaining its rounded corners.

However, if you had to ask me to pick between a Galaxy Z Fold8 running iOS and an iPhone Duo, I do think I would likely pick the Z Fold8. While I’d miss the rounded corners, the uninterrupted inner display experience, and a hinge that can hold itself in any position – having a lighter phone to carry around is an advantage, and the thinness makes a notable difference when you’re holding the phone closed.

#10094. How to Build an Accessible Multi Step Form: A Practical Guide With a Vanilla JavaScript Example

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A long form can feel like a chore before anyone types a word. Splitting it into smaller steps lets people focus on a few related questions at a time.

The code looks simple: show one section, hide the rest and add a button. The difficult parts are validation, keyboard use, focus and what happens when JavaScript doesn't run. This guide builds an accessible multi step form with HTML and plain JavaScript. It also reproduces a bug that can leave visitors stuck on the first step.

Multiple steps make sense when questions fall into clear groups, such as project details, timing and contact information. A form with only an email address and a message usually doesn't need them.

Splitting a form doesn't make unnecessary questions useful. Remove those first. More steps also aren't a guarantee of more completed forms, so test the change with your own audience.

The W3C tutorial on multiple page forms advises dividing long forms into logical stages and telling users about their progress. Our example applies those ideas within a single page.

Without JavaScript, all three groups remain visible and Send works as an ordinary submit button. The Back button stays hidden because it has no job in that view. This approach is called progressive enhancement: start with a working form, then add the step behavior.

The action value is a placeholder. Point it to a real server endpoint in your project. If your stylesheet sets a display value on fieldsets, check that it doesn't override the hidden attribute.

Save the following code as form.js in the same folder:

The form has one submit button. Its label reads Continue until the last step. The submit handler decides whether to show an error, open the next step or let the browser send the data. Pressing Enter in a text field follows the same path.

Back is a regular button, so it never submits anything. The answers remain in their fields because the code only hides each step. It doesn't remove or rebuild it.

To test its purpose, I removed only this line, filled in the first field and pressed Enter in headless Chromium 158. The form stayed on step 1. The console reported messages like these:

Before firing the submit event, the browser checked the entire form. The empty required fields on later steps were invalid, but they were hidden. Submission was blocked, so our handler never had a chance to open step 2.

Setting noValidate stops that automatic check during submission. It doesn't disable checkValidity() or reportValidity(), so our code can validate only the visible step. Because the line lives in JavaScript, the browser's normal validation still applies if the script never loads.

For more detail on native validation rules and methods, see SitePoint's Complete Guide to HTML Forms and Constraint Validation.

The test intercepted the request, so it checked what the browser sent rather than a real server. It also doesn't show how other browsers behave.

Each legend includes the current step number, and the page title changes with it. After someone moves to another step, focus goes to the new legend. This gives keyboard users a clear starting point. The code doesn't move focus during the first page load.

The negative tabindex lets JavaScript focus a legend without adding it to the normal Tab order. Keep the focus outline visible, or replace it with an equally clear style. Screen reader behavior varies, so test with the browser and screen reader combinations your visitors use.

Native validation messages are useful for a small example. A production form may need persistent error text beside each field. If so, connect each message to its field with aria-describedby, set aria-invalid when needed and clear both after the error is fixed.

Also avoid asking for the same information twice. WCAG 2.2 Redundant Entry requires previously entered information needed again in the same process to be filled in or available to select. There are exceptions, including certain security needs and information that is no longer valid. This example alone doesn't make a whole website WCAG compliant.

Browser checks can be changed or bypassed. Your server should validate required fields, allowed choices, lengths and formats again. It should also handle spam, protect against cross site request forgery where relevant and escape stored values when displaying them.

Before release, try the complete form with only a keyboard. Check mobile layouts, zoom, browser autofill, Back and Forward navigation, slow connections and server errors. Make sure repeated clicks don't create duplicate records.

SitePoint's practical workflow for testing JavaScript forms is a helpful next step when you're preparing a broader test plan.

The example also leaves out conditional questions. If one answer can remove a later step, calculate the active steps before updating the progress label. Decide how to handle old answers from inactive branches, and enforce the same rules on the server.

Custom code gives you control over the markup and behavior, but your team must maintain validation, delivery, security and testing. A form builder can reduce the amount of interface code you manage. For example, LeadGen App lists multi step forms, conditional logic and website embedding among its features.

Whichever route you choose, test the finished form instead of relying on a feature list.

An accessible multi step form needs more than a few hidden sections. Start with semantic HTML, validate each visible step and keep progress and focus clear.

Pay special attention to required fields that appear later. A single hidden field can stop the first button from working, even when the visible answer is correct. Test that case, then check the whole journey with real users and a real server before launch.

#10093. Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

As highlighted in our announcement post, we said that each new model generation lets agents do more on their own. However, the more an agent can reach, the more one bad decision can cost. You've probably seen a few of these in the news lately:

Because these events produced no system crashes or logging errors, the teams behind these agents had no way to know until the damage occurred.

Agent Anomaly Detection on the Gemini Platform watches for exactly these issues. It reads the traces and logs your agents already produce and flags sessions where an agent goes outside the job it was built for.

Before you point it at your production agents, you'll probably want to see it work in a test environment. In this hands-on guide, we’ll walk you step-by-step through setting up Agent Anomaly Detection and using it to capture your very first agent behavior anomaly. By the end of this post, you'll have:

To get started, open the companion notebook Detect anomalous agent behavior with Agent Anomaly Detection in a separate tab (available on Google Colab or GitHub). As you read through this guide, it will prompt you when to execute notebook cells or interact directly with the Google Cloud console.

If a step can be done in the UI console, this post shows that path, with screenshots. The notebook does the same thing through the Agent Anomaly Detection API, so by the end you'll also have the code to automate enablement and pull findings into your own tooling.

2. A place to run the notebook: Google Colab, Colab Enterprise, or a local Jupyter environment. The notebook installs the latest ADK for Python itself. Agent Anomaly Detection is compatible with agents running ADK 1.2+, though ADK 2.0+ is preferred for best results.

3. A Cloud Logging bucket and an Observability dataset in either US or EU multi-region. (Both resources need to be in the same multi-region)

4. In the notebook: run the Get started cells. They install the packages, authenticate you, take your project ID, and turn on the four APIs that Agent Anomaly Detection needs.

First, let’s create a Customer Support Agent called “anomaly-detection-refund-demo”. Our example agent is responsible for helping customers check their order history and request refunds. However, it has a deliberate flaw: the agent has access to an overprivileged fetch_records tool which can read any table, including a `payroll` table containing sensitive employee data. All three parts of the lethal trifecta are here: the agent can read private data, it takes input from people it can't vet, and it sends what it finds back in its reply. Tool permissions and guardrails should stop most of this, but they only block what someone thought to write down. Agent Anomaly Detection flags the sessions that get past them, usually within a few minutes.

The notebook has the full agent. The part that matters is the tool, which never checks the table name against an allowlist:

All data is canned and in memory, so nothing real is read or refunded. This tool is intended to be over-permissive to illustrate AAD functionality. Don't copy this tool into production.

The Step 1 cells deploy the agent to Agent Runtime. Two details in the deploy cell are what make the agent visible to Agent Anomaly Detection. First, it sets three environment variables on the deployment, which turn on telemetry and write prompt and response content to Cloud Logging, where detection looks for it:

Second, it sends the agent one test message right after deploying. Agent Anomaly Detection discovers agents through their telemetry, so an agent that has never served a request does not appear under Monitored agents.

The deployment takes about 5 to 10 minutes and prints the agent's resource name and the reply to the test message:

For more information on how to create and deploy an agent, take a look at these guides: Creating an Agent and Deploying an Agent.

In the console: The sample code we provide already enables logging and telemetry when deploying the agent. Verify this by navigating to the Agent Deployments page (Gemini Platform > Agents > Deployments) and clicking “Configure” next to the recently created anomaly-detection-refund-demo agent to open the Observability Service Configuration.

Great! You now have a working agent that is successfully configured and ready to be monitored by Agent Anomaly Detection.

Now let’s set up Agent Anomaly Detection to monitor your agent’s traffic.

In the console: navigate to the Agent Platform > Agents > Audit page and click “Enable”.

Choose your agent’s Logging Bucket and Observability Dataset, making sure they are in the same region (us-multi-region or eu-multi-region) enable “Automatically monitor new agents”, then click “Agree and continue” and Agent Anomaly Detection will be set up in your project.

If you'd rather do this in code, the first Step 2 cell in the notebook creates the same configuration through the API, using the default _Default bucket and _Trace dataset with automatic monitoring on.

Setup takes around 30 minutes. Once it’s done loading you’re ready to proceed to the next step! Because you enabled automatic monitoring and the agent already served a request in Step 1, setup picks it up right away. Check that it shows as Active under Settings > Monitored agents before you continue; sessions that start earlier aren't analyzed.

Now that the agent is being monitored, let's see Agent Anomaly Detection in action! The Step 3 cells send one normal conversation and one anomalous conversation, each in its own session. The normal conversation is a customer listing their orders and getting a refund for a broken lamp. The anomalous conversation simulates a user who finds the fetch_records flaw and uses it to pull payroll data:

Notice that the user brings almost nothing: a customer ID and one first name. Asking for a table that doesn't exist makes the agent reveal the existing tables, which is how they learn there is a payroll table within reach.

The notebook prints both transcripts with the agent's tool calls, and the session IDs you'll look up next.

Anomaly Detection will scan the sessions within 5 minutes. In the landing page, you should see that a couple sessions have been processed and one anomalous session detected.

Let’s take a look at the anomalous session in more detail. Click the session id to open the detail view or paste the anomalous session id into the search bar.

In our run, the top finding was Tool misuse at High severity, with 95% probability. Your detectors and severities may differ, because the models behind the analysis are non-deterministic. The recommended solution is to restrict the `fetch_records` tool to read from specific tables.

Click an invocation in the Session pane to see its analysis in Details. Each invocation is Medium on its own, but together they make the session High.

To stay informed without watching the console, connect Agent Anomaly Detection to your existing alerting workflows.

In Cloud Monitoring: Agent Anomaly Detection publishes two metrics: Detected events, which counts anomalies by detector and severity, and Monitored agents, which tracks your detection coverage. To view these metrics, click the arrow next to "View in Cloud Monitoring" on the Anomaly detection page and select a metric to open it in Metrics Explorer.

To be notified of new anomalies, go to Monitoring > Alerting, click "Create policy", and select the Detected events metric. Leave the filters empty so the alert covers every anomaly, including the demo's, then set the rolling window to 5 minutes with the delta function, and set the threshold to above 0. Once you know what's normal for your agents, add a severity filter (for example High or Critical) so you're only alerted on the findings that matter. A metric-absence policy on Monitored agents will also alert you if monitoring stops.

In Security Command Center: Each anomaly is also published as an AGENT_SESSION_ANOMALY finding in the Security Command Center if it is enabled for your organization. Click "View in Security Command Center" to triage these findings alongside your other threats, use "Set security marks" to tag and track them, and set up notifications for high-severity findings.

You’ve now seen Agent Anomaly Detection in action: it successfully flagged the session where our agent went off-script to read payroll records, while evaluating the benign session, and correctly not flagging an anomaly. The finding told you why it was flagged and how to fix the underlying issue, and alerts in Cloud Monitoring and Security Command Center ensure you are notified of future anomalies.

When you're ready to secure your own production workloads, follow this rollout plan:

#10087. Node.js Hosting Between Serverless and VPS: Predictable Costs Without Cold Starts

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The first sign of trouble is rarely a crash. It is the invoice.

A small team ships a Node.js app on a serverless platform because it is quick to set up. Traffic grows, and the bill grows with it, in ways that are hard to forecast. Some visitors also wait a beat longer for their first response, because the function has to start up before it can answer.

The usual alternative is a virtual private server (VPS). The price is fixed, but the work is not. Someone has to patch the operating system (OS), configure the web server, renew SSL certificates and keep backups running.

Between the two sits a third option: managed hosting for Node.js. Here is how the three compare, and how to choose.

Platforms such as AWS Lambda charge for what runs. Lambda's event functions are priced on the number of requests and on duration, measured in GB-seconds, and the memory you choose sets the CPU and the cost per second.

That suits small or spiky workloads. A new app with little traffic can sit inside the free tier, which covers one million requests and 400,000 GB-seconds a month.

The surprises come with growth. Billed duration is multiplied by the number of requests, so even small inefficiencies in a handler can turn into significant costs at scale.

Then there is the cold start. When the platform has to create a new execution environment, the first request waits. AWS's own decision guide puts Lambda cold starts at roughly 100 milliseconds to over 1 second.

Since August 2025, AWS bills for that initialization (INIT) phase across all function configurations, so a cold start now shows up on the invoice as well as in the response time.

AWS's own analysis found they occur in under 1% of invocations, so steady traffic makes them far less. For a checkout page that does hit one, it is still a pause the customer feels.

There are fixes. Provisioned concurrency keeps functions initialized and ready, but it is billed for the time it is enabled. Newer options let one execution environment handle several requests at once, which means fewer cold starts.

Limits matter, too. A Lambda function can run for 15 minutes at most, and accounts start with a default of 1,000 concurrent executions. Long-running jobs need a different home.

A VPS fixes the pricing problem. A plan costs the same every month, whatever traffic does.

On an unmanaged VPS, the team owns everything above the hardware. That means operating system updates, a firewall, a web server such as NGINX in front of the app, a process manager such as PM2 to restart it if it crashes, SSL certificate renewals and backups that someone has to test.

None of it is exotic. All of it is a job. Left undone, it is how small teams end up with unpatched servers and no way to restore one.

For a team without a sysadmin, the real price of a VPS is the hours.

Managed Node.js hosting sits between the two. The app runs on a persistent infrastructure, so there is no scale-to-zero and no cold start between requests. The price is a flat monthly plan. The provider handles the work around it.

Cloudways Velocity is one example. It patches the OS, manages SSL and domain mapping, and runs backups automatically.

The stack is NGINX and PM2, pre-configured with caching, and PostgreSQL is provisioned on all plans. Deployment is by Git push with built-in CI/CD, and a WAF, malware protection, DDoS mitigation and Cloudflare Enterprise CDN are included.

It ships presets for 12 project types, including Next.js, Remix, Express, Fastify, Astro, Nuxt, Angular and n8n. Anything that builds to SSR or a static bundle and starts with a Node command can run under "Other". Runtime versions are selectable, with Node.js LTS 22.x and 24.x listed.

The trade-off is scaling. Velocity scales vertically: you pick a server size and move up when the app outgrows it. A traffic spike beyond that server's capacity is a capacity problem, not something the platform absorbs by adding instances.

Look at the traffic first. If it comes in bursts with long quiet gaps, serverless often wins. If it is steady, a server is usually simpler to reason about.

Take one handler and keep the assumptions the same throughout. Each request runs for half a second on 1 GB of memory. The rates are AWS's published x86 prices: $0.20 per million requests and $0.0000166667 per GB-second, after the free tier, as shown earlier.

Now compare Velocity's plans, which use a pay-as-you-go model billed on an hourly basis according to the selected plan, ranging from Starter at $20 a month (2 GB RAM, 2 vCPU, 5 apps) to Business at $400 (64 GB RAM, 16 vCPU, 150 apps). More importantly billing is strictly tied to the server plan rather than incoming traffic, a sudden spike in visits will not automatically raise your hourly compute rate.

The Lambda figures leave out API gateway charges, data transfer and logging. A single server has a ceiling, so load-test before assuming one plan carries 100 million requests.

And Velocity has metered extras: $0.033/GB for offsite backup storage, $0.02/GB for CDN bandwidth beyond the plan's allocation, and $1 per 10 GB of extra disk.

If you want to test managed hosting, you can try Cloudways Velocity during Black Friday with the promo code BFCM802 for 80% off for 2 months plus unlimited migrations, valid through December 4, 2026.

The point is the shape, not a winner. Serverless cost tracks usage. A server's cost tracks its size. Choose the one you can forecast.

The strongest case is also the most common one: steady traffic, a small team and a budget that has to be forecast.

When cloud hosting costs have to fit a budget, a flat monthly plan beats a meter. Velocity's plans give you Node.js hosting with predictable pricing, so finance can see the number in advance and it does not move with every spike.

Because the server stays on, there is no cold start between requests, and the first visitor after a quiet spell is not left waiting.

Cloudways says Velocity patches the operating system, manages SSL and domain mapping, and runs backups automatically. The people who built the product keep building it.

APIs, full-stack apps and automation workflows such as n8n need a process that stays up. A persistent server is built for exactly that.

When the app outgrows its server, you move up a size, and you can move back down if you don't need it.

For most small teams, that adds up to a simple choice. Velocity trades the theoretical ceiling of serverless for something many teams value more: costs they can forecast and an app that stays ready, without a DevOps hire.

Teams whose traffic is extreme, or who need code running at the edge, may still prefer a specialist platform, but that is the exception.

Serverless is strongest when work is irregular. A VPS is strongest when you want control and have the time. Managed Node.js hosting suits the large group in between: teams with steady traffic, a small headcount and no appetite for server upkeep.

Teams looking for managed hosting can explore Cloudways Velocity to deploy and scale Node.js applications efficiently.

He works closely with different teams to uncover the most genuine user experiences, then uses his precise understanding of narrative and language to shape these stories. His focus is on ensuring every story is not just authentic, but also clearly communicates the real-world value of the platform, building a trustworthy bridge between the customer's voice and the company's message.

Over the past month, Gemini 4 Argon and Gemini 4 Carbon brought frontier physical reasoning to robotics: understanding 3D scenes, reconstructing real environments in simulation, practicing tasks in sim before running them on hardware, and directly controlling robot arms from camera images. #10074. How to Shut Down a Node.js API Without Dropping Requests

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every deploy, autoscaling event and node drain ends the same way for a running Node.js process: the platform sends it SIGTERM and waits. What happens next decides whether your users notice the deploy.

If the process has no handler for that signal, it exits on the spot. Any request that was halfway through, say a report that takes three seconds of database work, never gets a response. Here is what that looks like with a plain Express app and no signal handling:

Exit code 143 is 128 plus 15, the number of SIGTERM: the process was killed by the signal rather than exiting on its own. The client gets an empty reply, and if that request was writing data, nobody knows whether the write finished.

A graceful shutdown fixes this by draining the process: it stops taking new work, finishes what it already started, closes its connections and then exits. This tutorial builds that sequence for an Express 5 API backed by PostgreSQL, tests it against a real database, and covers the container and Kubernetes details that decide whether the signal ever reaches your code.

The order of operations matters more than any single line of code. A process that receives SIGTERM should go through four steps:

The first step is the one most implementations skip. If the server closes immediately, requests that the load balancer routes in the next second or two get connection errors, which is exactly what graceful shutdown was supposed to prevent.

Create an ES module project and install Express 5 and node-postgres:

Two timings drive the whole sequence. DRAIN_DELAY_MS is how long the process keeps serving after it fails readiness. It should be longer than your load balancer's health-check interval multiplied by its failure threshold. SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS is the hard deadline for the whole shutdown, and it must be shorter than the time your platform waits before sending SIGKILL.

The sleep helper comes from Node's promise-based timers API, so the shutdown code can await a delay without wrapping setTimeout by hand.

The middleware handles a detail that is easy to miss. HTTP clients reuse connections with keep-alive, so a client that already has a socket open to this instance would keep sending requests down it. A Connection: close header on the response tells the client to close that socket and open a new one, which the load balancer will route to a healthy instance.

Liveness stays green during shutdown. The process isn't broken; it's leaving on purpose, and a failing liveness probe could get it restarted in the middle of draining.

Shutdown code rarely gets tested, because it only runs on deploys. You can test it locally with curl and kill. Start the server with a short drain delay, start the slow request, and send SIGTERM while it is running:

Here is the timeline from a run against PostgreSQL 16 on Node.js 22:

Everything the sequence promised shows up in order. Readiness failed within a third of a second, the instance kept answering during the drain delay, new connections were refused once the server closed, and the request that was already running finished with a 200 before the process exited cleanly. Compare that with the empty reply and exit code 143 at the start of this article.

All of this only works if SIGTERM actually arrives at the Node.js process. In containers, that is not a given.

The Dockerfile reference explains the difference between the shell and exec forms. With the shell form, CMD node server.js, Docker starts /bin/sh -c as the main process, and the shell does not pass SIGTERM on to Node.js. Your handler never runs, docker stop waits out its grace period (10 seconds by default), and then the container is killed. For the same reason, start node directly rather than through an npm script or a shell wrapper, so there is no extra process between the platform and your code.

The first process in a container runs as PID 1, which the Linux kernel treats specially: signals whose default action is to terminate are ignored unless the process installs a handler. The code above installs handlers, so Node.js shuts down correctly as PID 1. But any code path that relies on default signal behavior won't, and PID 1 also has to reap orphaned child processes. Running the container with docker run --init puts a tiny init process in front of Node.js that forwards signals and reaps zombies, and it costs nothing to add.

When Kubernetes deletes a pod, it removes the pod from Service endpoints and sends SIGTERM at roughly the same time, not one after the other. That race is the reason for the drain delay: for a few seconds after the signal, traffic can still arrive. After the grace period, 30 seconds by default, the kubelet sends SIGKILL. The pod termination documentation describes the full sequence, including preStop hooks, which run before SIGTERM and count toward the same grace period.

The timings need to nest inside each other:

With a 2-second readiness period and a failure threshold of 1, a 5-second drain delay leaves a comfortable margin for the pod to leave rotation. The 20-second shutdown deadline sits well inside the 30-second grace period, so the process exits on its own terms instead of being killed. If your slowest legitimate request takes longer than about 15 seconds, raise terminationGracePeriodSeconds along with the deadline rather than letting those requests be cut off.

Graceful shutdown is about forty lines of code, and it runs on every single deploy. Getting it right means a release is something your users never notice, which is exactly how a release should feel.

#10063. Accelerating development for massive Git repositories

Android’s source code is big – the Android 17 open source release has 1,084 Git repositories, 1.62 million files, and takes 300 GB for a built workspace. Additionally, in a typical Android source tree with hardware vendor code and first-party applications, the size increases by 2-3x. Traditionally, it took over 30 minutes to fetch Android Open Source Project (AOSP) source code and well over an hour to build on even highly capable developer workstations. As software development evolved over the last 2 years, modern coding harnesses and LLMs have accelerated the development of many projects. These tools are used today for Android operating system development, but the long sync and build times make them less efficient. Both human developers and agents need quick setup, parallel workspaces, and fast build-and-test iteration times.

We built Another Build File Service (ABFS) to address these bottlenecks. ABFS is a full suite of software across a Linux kernel filesystem module, user-space daemons, and server-side infrastructure. ABFS uses content-addressability across the entire stack to lazy-load and cache source code, developer workspaces, intermediate build files, and build results.

Today, we are excited to announce that ABFS is open source on GitHub.The impact of ABFS to Android is:

ABFS already powers parallel workflows for developers and agents in Android, but it is designed to scale to other massive Git-based projects.

In the Android build system, developers define build rules in Blueprint files (Android.bp), Soong compiles them into Ninja files, and Ninja executes the final build graph (see the Android build overview). Similar to the Make build tool, Ninja is timestamp-based. If a build step’s input file is newer than the existing output file, Ninja rebuilds the output file.

Analyzing the AOSP code for Android 17 shows the sheer disk footprint and file count involved in a single checkout and build.

In a typical Android tree that includes hardware-specific repositories, board support packages, first-party applications, form-factor-specific code, etc., the code size is 2–3 times the size of AOSP (over 3,000 Git repositories), compounded across multiple active SKU and legacy maintenance branches.

Building the Cuttlefish emulator target executes a build graph with 192,249 Ninja actions. While local incremental builds are fast once a tree is already built, every new workspace, branch switch, or ephemeral agent session starts cold.Even though typical developer changes touch only 1-10 different Git projects, someone building locally without any remote execution might waste up to 80 minutes on a 64-core machine repeating work their CI system already built. Even if a developer edits a core library like system/libbase, 97% of the build graph is identical to the CI build.

Sharing cached build outputs across CI and local machines is also tricky in a codebase of Android's age and rate of change. Build rules occasionally introduce hidden dependencies (such as passing an undeclared schema file inside raw compiler flags), over-declared inputs, or undeclared outputs. In timestamp-based builds, hidden dependencies silently produce stale incremental artifacts, forcing developers to wipe output directories and run clean builds.

ABFS makes building Android fast by replacing massive local source checkouts with a virtual, content-addressable filesystem (CASFS). ABFS streams files on demand only when a build actually touches them and utilizes shared build caching to help eliminate redundant downloads and compilation.

On the server side, Git-pushers continuously ingest new Git objects from Gerrit or other Git hosts into a horizontally scalable gRPC ABFS Content-Addressable Storage (CAS) Server that indexes Git content, workspace snapshots, and build step results. On the developer host, a local Least Recently Used (LRU) Cacheman daemon, Mount Daemon, and Tap utility connect user-space build tools to the kernel filesystem so checkouts and builds stream only what they need. See the full ABFS architecture on GitHub.

At the core of the client is a virtual filesystem called Content-Addressable Storage Filesystem (CASFS), a Linux kernel module that exposes Git SHA content hashes via extended attributes. When a file's hash is set, CASFS populates its metadata immediately and downloads file bytes via Cacheman only when needed:

Initial checkouts download only lightweight directory metadata prepopulated with Git hashes. CASFS uses Git's object model natively, allowing standard git and repo operations to work transparently.

CASFS can also tap build processes. Tapping records every file actually read, written, or deleted into lists called patches, which are stored alongside cached build outputs. Ninja-go replaces timestamp checks with two-phase content-addressable caching:

Ninja-go accelerates Ninja-based builds like Android and Chrome; other build systems can integrate with ABFS's caching APIs using the same model (see the two-phase caching walkthrough on GitHub).

Each mounted checkout attaches to a persistent workspace that eagerly syncs modified files, git state, and repo state through Cacheman to the ABFS CAS Server—keeping mounts synchronized across sessions or across multiple developer hosts.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The benchmarks below show the mean time of 3 Android runs on a GCE n2d-standard-64 VM with 64 vCPUs and 256 GB of RAM. The ABFS remote server was running in the same cloud network as the client.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

Because ABFS does lazy loading and local cache deduplication, developers and their agents can now mount multiple concurrent Android workspaces on a single machine, benefit from a shared local build cache, and achieve massive scale in development time.

An agent can check out a full Android tree in under 10 seconds and perform a full build in under 10 minutes. Agent swarms are empowered to iterate, review, and test in parallel, which can lead to higher quality solutions and speed.In the demo below, an agent makes a system/libbase fix on the latest AOSP release, then 4 parallel agents simultaneously backport, build, and test the fix across 4 more AOSP releases on a single VM.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

We released ABFS as open source because we want developers, Android partners, and any large-scale Git-based project to benefit from what we have learned and be able to scale.

While Android’s Soong/Ninja pipeline was the first acceleration target, ABFS was built to be extensible to other build systems. Content-addressable build systems like Bazel map naturally onto CASFS and there is already a proof-of-concept integration with Bazel. Chromium, the Android Common Kernel, and other Google projects are exploring ABFS compatibility.

Explore ABFS, CASFS, and Ninja-go on GitHub, and reach out to us at abfs@google.com.

For help with the Google Cloud server-side, infrastructure-as-code, and CASFS for Cloud Workstations and Kubernetes, please reach out to abfs-gcp@google.com.

We’d like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

Haamed Gheibi, Erik Sanders, Rob Seymour, Taylor Santiago, Andrew Hsieh, Eric Taseski, Kiyoung Kim, Hung-Wei Chen, Jose “Pepe” Galmes, Ronald Li, Yu-Che Wang, Roger Ellis, Sandeep Patil, Amith Dsouza and the entire Android Build team

#10062. Beyond Smartphones: How AI Is Shaping the Next Generation of Personal Devices (Developer Guide)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Smartphones have defined personal computing for nearly two decades. They changed how people communicate, work, navigate and find information. They also still depend on screens, app switching and a lot of manual effort.

Artificial intelligence is starting to change that relationship. Emerging personal devices can respond to natural language, use context and offer help when it is needed instead of waiting for constant input. For developers, that raises practical questions: where should a model run, what should an interface look like without a large screen, and how much data should leave the device? This article walks through those questions and includes a small, tested helper for one of them. Product details change quickly, so check each platform's current documentation.

The smartphone interaction model is screen-centered: open an app, navigate menus, enter a command. AI-powered devices combine machine learning, sensors and conversational interfaces to interpret a request with fewer manual steps.

Navigation is a simple example. On a phone, you check the map or listen to directions. A wearable could give timely audio instructions or a visual cue based on location and movement. That does not mean the phone goes away. Personal computing may instead spread across interconnected devices, with the phone continuing to supply connectivity and processing.

Contextual computing means a system uses relevant information about the environment, user activity or earlier interactions. A traditional app responds to explicit commands. An AI-assisted one can also interpret natural-language requests and combine several inputs. A personal assistant might use calendar and location data, with permission, to suggest when someone should leave for an appointment.

Multimodal AI, which McKinsey describes as AI that can handle several kinds of data at once, including text, images, audio and video, makes interactions feel more natural than touch controls alone.

Context only helps when the underlying information is reliable. Developers need to manage permissions, data accuracy and user expectations, or the feature will feel intrusive or wrong.

Smartwatches and fitness trackers already showed the value of getting information without pulling out a phone. AI extends this to other products, including smart eyewear, earbuds and connected accessories.

For interaction design, the point is to deliver help while the user keeps doing something else. That changes what an app should show, say and ask.

Many AI features rely on cloud infrastructure. The cloud offers large models and plenty of compute, but adds network delay and requires sending data off the device. On-device AI runs supported models on personal hardware. It can reduce latency, support some offline features and limit how much sensitive information is transmitted.

Local processing has costs. Small devices have limited memory, battery and thermal headroom, so teams balance model size, compute demand and response quality. Hybrid architectures split the work between local hardware and cloud services, and are a practical answer for many applications.

A hybrid app needs a rule for where each request runs. The Python module below is a deliberately simple example. It takes the task (model size, whether the data is sensitive, the latency the user will tolerate) and the device state (free memory, battery, connectivity, measured round-trip time) and returns device, cloud, ask_user or unavailable.

The tests cover the main cases: a live caption on glasses stays on the device when the network is too slow, a large model that does not fit goes to the cloud, sensitive data stays local or triggers a question to the user when the model will not fit, an offline device runs only what fits, and low battery pushes a heavy job to the cloud.

Ambient computing describes technology that blends into everyday environments instead of demanding continuous attention. Users may reach connected services through speakers, watches, glasses and other devices. Someone could start a task with a voice assistant, get a reminder on a watch and continue on a laptop without recreating the context.

That requires reliable synchronization, interoperable services and consistent identity across devices. AI can help interpret requests and coordinate interactions, but a seamless experience still depends on well-designed software architecture. Developers should decide how information moves between devices, what happens when connectivity fails and how users stay in control of automated actions.

Apps built around large screens and touch navigation may not translate well to devices that rely on speech, audio feedback or compact visual displays. Google's Android XR developer documentation describes building for several device types, including XR headsets, audio glasses and display glasses, and lists tools such as the Jetpack XR SDK, Unity, OpenXR and WebXR.

Whatever the platform, a few priorities recur:

Not every interaction benefits from AI. Predictable tasks may be better served by a conventional interface, so use intelligent features where they measurably improve usability.

Probably not soon. Phones are likely to remain a hub for connectivity and processing, while wearables and ambient devices handle some everyday interactions.

Running a machine learning model directly on personal hardware, so a task can run without sending each request to a remote server.

When the model is too large for the device, the data is not sensitive and the network is fast enough. Many apps use a hybrid, as in the routing helper above.

Interaction relies more on voice and audio, displays are small or absent, and messages must be short and timely because the user is busy doing something else.
